## THE CONFORMALLY INVARIANT METRIC ON CLE 4 III: UNIQUENESS

EMMANUEL KAMMERER, KONSTANTINOS KAVVADIAS, JASON MILLER AND YI TIAN

Abstract. This paper is the third and final article in a series of papers constructing the canonical conformally invariant metric on the set of loops of the conformal loop ensemble (CLE) with critical parameter κ = 4. The previous two articles construct, as a subsequential limit of the renormalized graph metric on the loops of CLE κ as κ ↓ 4, a conformally invariant, local metric on the loops of a CLE 4 whose metric ball growth from the domain boundary coincides with the uniform exploration of Werner and Wu. In this paper, we establish that this metric is uniquely characterized by its properties, as are its geodesics, and that it is a measurable function of the CLE 4 . In particular, we show that the renormalized CLE κ graph metric converges as κ ↓ 4 without passing to a subsequence. A key step in the proof is to show that the metric is determined by the geodesics from each loop to the domain boundary, which are in turn determined by the uniform exploration; this representation will have important applications in future work.

## 1. Introduction

1.1. Overview. The conformal loop ensemble (CLE κ ) with parameter κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 8) is a random collection of non-crossing loops within a simply connected domain in C [She09, SW12]. The CLE κ is conformally invariant, meaning its law is preserved under conformal automorphisms of the domain. Its law in an arbitrary simply connected domain is then defined by pushing forward under a conformal map, so that conformal invariance holds for all conformal maps between such domains. CLEs are the loop variant of the Schramm-Loewner evolution (SLE) [Sch00]. SLEs and CLEs are conjectured (and in several cases rigorously shown) to describe the scaling limits of interfaces in critical statistical mechanics models on planar lattices [Smi01, LSW04, SS09, Smi10] as well as on random planar maps [She16, LSW24, GKMW18, KMSW19, GM21a, GM21c].

Just like for SLE, the value κ = 4 is critical for CLE. On the one hand, for κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4], the loops are simple and do not intersect each other or the domain boundary. On the other hand, for κ ∈ (4 , 8), the loops are self-intersecting, intersect each other, and also the domain boundary [RS05]. In this paper, we focus on the critical case κ = 4. For κ &gt; 4, since the loops intersect, there is a natural distance that one can define on the set of loops of a CLE κ . It is simply the graph metric where the vertices of the graph are the loops of the CLE κ and there is an edge precisely when two loops intersect. Since a CLE κ is conformally invariant and this metric is a function of a CLE κ , the metric is trivially conformally invariant. This construction does not make sense when κ = 4 since the loops do not intersect each other. Nevertheless, we proved in [KKMT26a, KKMT26b] that such a conformally invariant metric on the loops of a CLE 4 does exist and that it is a geodesic metric. The main aim of this paper is to show that this conformally invariant metric is unique. See Figure 1 for numerical simulations of the metric.

The program of constructing the CLE 4 metric was initiated by Werner and Wu in [WW13], in which the distance from any loop in the CLE 4 to the domain boundary was constructed. Equivalently, they gave a definition of the growth of a metric ball from the domain boundary, where one discovers each loop at a time corresponding to its distance from the domain boundary. They called this the uniform exploration (cf. also [WW17, AS18]). The reason for this terminology is that as one performs the uniform exploration from the domain boundary, the CLE 4 loops are discovered in a Poissonian way, each new loop being rooted at a point chosen according to the harmonic measure on the boundary of the explored region infinitesimally before the time at which it appears. It was not proved in [WW13] that the uniform exploration corresponds to the growth of the metric balls from the boundary for a metric defined on the entire CLE 4 . This is one of the properties of the metric constructed in [KKMT26a, KKMT26b]. It was also conjectured in [WW13] that the uniform exploration is a.s. determined by the CLE 4 . In this third paper, we prove this conjecture. More precisely, we establish the uniqueness of the metric constructed in [KKMT26a, KKMT26b], whose metric balls correspond to the uniform exploration, as well as the fact that the metric is a.s. determined by the CLE 4 .

Theorem 1.1 (See Theorem 1.5 for a precise statement) . There exists a unique geodesic metric on the set of loops of the CLE 4 which is local and conformally invariant, and whose metric balls from the domain boundary correspond to the uniform exploration. Moreover, this metric is a measurable function of the CLE 4 .

The metric constructed in this series of articles was also obtained by Sheffield, Watson, and Wu in unpublished work. In contrast to the indirect approach taken by Sheffield, Watson, and Wu, we already showed in [KKMT26a] that the metric arises as an appropriately renormalized limit as κ ↓ 4 of the adjacency graph metric of CLE κ loops. Moreover, in [KKMT26b], we proved the existence of geodesics: this question was not tackled by Sheffield, Watson, and Wu. In this paper, one of the main steps in establishing the uniqueness of the metric will be to show that the CLE 4 distance between arbitrary loops can be represented in terms of the uniform exploration. In particular, we prove that geodesics can be approximated by going 'up and down' in the geodesics to the boundary. This representation will have important consequences for future work, which we will describe in more detail just below.

We anticipate that the CLE 4 metric will have applications in the study of random planar maps. Recall that a planar map is a graph together with an embedding into the plane such that no two edges cross. Two planar maps are considered to be equivalent if it is possible to deform one into the other using an orientation-preserving homeomorphism of the plane. In recent years, the asymptotic behavior of random planar maps has received a large amount of attention. Major results include the Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov convergence of rescaled quadrangulations toward the Brownian sphere [MM06, Mie13, LG13] and of Boltzmann planar maps with large faces toward the α -stable carpet or gasket [LGM11, CMR25]. In this general framework, we expect that our random metric space describes the scaling limit of random maps with high degree vertices called 3 / 2-stable maps. These are the dual maps of random planar maps with large faces, where faces of degree 2 k have a weight of order c k k - 2 for the appropriate critical constant c . They were first studied in the local limit in [BCM18]. Even though these maps do not admit a scaling limit in the sense of GromovHausdorff or Gromov-Prokhorov [Kam25b], a non-trivial scaling limit of the distances to the root has been uncovered by one of the authors in [Kam25a] which can be identified with a distance from the loops of the CLE 4 to the boundary introduced in [AHPS23], and which, in future work, will be shown to agree with the metric constructed here.

As shown in [Kam26] (and in [ADSH26] in the particular case of quadrangulations, see also [DHPW25] for the solvable fully-packed case on triangulations), the gasket of a critical O (2) loopdecorated planar map, obtained by forgetting the parts of the map that are inside the outermost loops, is an instance of a 3 / 2-stable map. Our expectation is thus consistent with the conjecture that the scaling limit of critical O (2) loop-decorated maps is described by the CLE 4 together with an independent critical Liouville quantum gravity (see e.g. [HL24, Conjecture 2.1] for a precise conjecture).

The scaling limits of the random maps with large faces mentioned above (which are often called α -stable maps for α ∈ (1 , 2)) are known as the α -stable carpets, or gaskets when α &lt; 3 / 2. In this terminology, our metric space can be seen as the 'dual' of the 3 / 2-stable carpet. We also expect that it is possible to construct analogous duals for the (1 / 2 + 4 /κ )-stable carpets, where κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4), corresponding to a metric on the loops of a CLE κ , though they would be conformally covariant instead of conformally invariant.

1.2. Uniqueness of limits of random metric spaces. Identifying the limit of a sequence of random metric spaces is a central question in the field of random geometry. Sometimes, the limit can be characterized as a function of a stochastic process. The prototypical example is the Brownian tree [Ald91], which can be obtained by gluing together those pairs of points on the graph of a Brownian excursion that can be connected by a horizontal chord staying below the graph of the excursion. Similarly, the Brownian sphere, defined in [MM06], can be obtained as a function of the Brownian snake. A similar construction was obtained in [CMR25] for the scaling limit of random maps with large faces. In the same spirit, in this paper, we describe the conformally invariant metric on the CLE 4 as a function of the uniform exploration introduced in [WW13] (moreover, we also establish that the uniform exploration is determined by the CLE 4 ).

Yet, defining a candidate for the limiting metric space is often not sufficient to establish the uniqueness of the limit. A deeper understanding of the geometry is particularly useful for that purpose. In their independent proofs of the uniqueness of the Brownian sphere, Le Gall [LG13] and Miermont [Mie13] both heavily use properties of geodesics. More precisely, [LG13] shows that geodesics can be approximated by going up and down in the tree of geodesics to a distinguished point. An analogous argument was carried out in [CMR25] for random maps with large faces.

In the study of random metric spaces having a natural embedding in the complex plane, the approach for proving uniqueness is different. While still relying on properties of geodesics, it makes heavy use of the embedding itself. For example, to prove the uniqueness of the Liouville quantum gravity metric, [GM21b, DG23] combine properties of geodesics with the independence across scales of the Gaussian free field (GFF) and the bi-Lipschitz equivalence of two conditionally independent samples of the metric given the GFF. The same pair of ideas (a bi-Lipschitz comparison of two conditionally independent samples of the metric, together with a suitable independence across scales) is also important for the proofs of the uniqueness of the conformally covariant metric on the simple CLE carpet [MT25], of the conformally covariant metric on the non-simple CLE gasket [MY25b], and of the conformally covariant resistance form, and hence of the canonical Brownian motion, on the non-simple CLE gasket [MY25a].

In the case of the metric on the loops of a CLE 4 , one also has a natural embedding into the complex plane. However, there are significant challenges in making use of this embedding. First, it is more difficult to compare the metric with the Euclidean metric and its variants. Second, unlike in the works mentioned above, the metric transforms in a conformally invariant rather than conformally covariant manner. As a result, the arguments used in the aforementioned works to establish the bi-Lipschitz equivalence of conditionally independent samples do not carry over, leading us to take a different approach. In order to circumvent these issues, this paper instead borrows ideas from both frameworks: from the latter, we make use of the independence properties of CLE 4 and its coupling with the GFF, and as in the former [LG13, Mie13, CMR25], we prove that geodesics can be approximated by geodesics to the domain boundary, which we shall also refer to as the root . This was one of the key ingredients in the identification of the scaling limit of quadrangulations [LG13, Mie13] and of random maps with large faces [CMR25]. In the same way, we expect that this property will be crucial in order to obtain the scaling limit of 3 / 2-stable maps.

- 1.4. Main results. In [KKMT26a], we proved that subsequential limits of the CLE κ together with its rescaled graph distance as κ ↓ 4 give rise to a weak geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling. Then, in [KKMT26b], we proved that every weak geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling in fact admits geodesics; more precisely, any such coupling is a geodesic metric coupling according to the following definition.

Definition 1.2. We define a geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling to be a family of couplings

Note that, in the above definition, the metric D U Γ U is not required to be a function of Γ U . We will actually show that any geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling can be described as a uniquely defined measurable function of the CLE 4 . We thus introduce the notion of a strong CLE 4 metric, which is essentially a geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling such that the metric D U Γ U is a measurable function of Γ U . The coupling (Γ U , D U Γ U ) can then be recovered from Γ U together with the measurable map D U . More precisely, let us define a strong CLE 4 metric in the following way.

Remark 1.4 . We record the following two properties.

- In Axiom (II), the internal metric D U Γ U ( · , · ; V j ) is a.s. determined by V j and Γ U | V j .

Next, we state the main results of the paper. We prove that any geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling in the sense of Definition 1.2 coincides a.s. with a strong CLE 4 metric in the sense of Definition 1.3 and that such a strong CLE 4 metric is unique.

- Theorem 1.5 (Uniqueness of the metric) . (i) Let D be a geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling. Then there is a strong CLE 4 metric ˜ D such that for every simply connected open subset U ⊊ C , D U Γ U = ˜ D U Γ U a.s.
- (ii) If D and ˜ D are two strong CLE 4 metrics, then for each deterministic simply connected domain U ⊊ C we have D U Γ U = ˜ D U Γ U a.s.

One of the main consequences of the existence of the strong CLE 4 metric is the following corollary. It states that the uniform CLE 4 exploration introduced in [WW13] is a.s. determined by the associated CLE 4 , which answers [WW13, Question 3]. Moreover, it states that if the CLE 4 is coupled with a family of BCLE 4 ( ρ ) processes for ρ ∈ ( - 2 , 0) as in Section 2.8 (see also [KKMT26a, Section 2.7] for more details), then the BCLE 4 ( ρ )'s are a.s. determined by the associated CLE 4 .

Corollary 1.6 (Measurability) . Let U ⊊ C be a deterministic simply connected domain. Then:

- (i) The uniform exploration of Γ U is a.s. determined by Γ U .

(ii) Let Γ U and a family of BCLE 4 ( ρ ) 's for ρ ∈ ( - 2 , 0) be coupled as in Section 2.8. Then the BCLE 4 ( ρ ) 's are a.s. determined by Γ U .

̸

Theorem 1.7 (Convergence of non-simple CLE graph distance) . The following is true for every simply connected domain U ⊊ C . The random metrics a - 1 κ D U Γ κ U converge in law, with respect to the product topology of R U Q × U Q , to D U Γ U as κ ↓ 4 , where D is the strong CLE 4 metric of Theorem 1.5.

Finally, we state the last main results of the paper concerning the behavior of geodesics of the strong CLE 4 metric. The first of these states that, a.s., any two CLE 4 loops are connected by a unique geodesic. The same holds for geodesics between a deterministic boundary arc and a loop, and between two deterministic boundary arcs.

̸

̸

Theorem 1.8 (Uniqueness of geodesics) . Let U ⊊ C be a deterministic simply connected domain. Then, a.s., for each ℒ 1 , ℒ 2 ∈ Γ U , the D U Γ U -geodesic connecting ℒ 1 and ℒ 2 is unique in the following sense: if P 1 and P 2 are D U Γ U -geodesics connecting ℒ 1 and ℒ 2 , then they visit the same set of loops, that is, { ℒ ∈ Γ U : ℒ ∩ P 1 = ∅} = { ℒ ∈ Γ U : ℒ ∩ P 2 = ∅} . The same is true with a deterministic connected arc (resp. two deterministic disjoint connected arcs) of ∂U in place of ℒ 1 (resp. ℒ 1 and ℒ 2 ).

Another result, which we obtain in the proof of Theorem 1.5, states that geodesics between two loops can be approximated using geodesics to the boundary. More precisely, the distance between two loops ℒ 1 and ℒ 2 can be approximated using a sequence of loops ℒ 1 = ℒ ′ 0 , . . . , ℒ ′ n = ℒ 2 and some paths P i between ℒ ′ i - 1 and ℒ ′ i for each 1 ≤ i ≤ n such that for any loop ℒ visited by P i at some time t ∈ [0 , 1], there is a geodesic from ℒ to ∂U containing either the time-reversal of P i | [0 ,t ] or the path P i | [ t, 1] . See Proposition 3.4 for more details.

1.5. Outline. Next, we will give an outline of the content of the rest of the paper.

Section 2: Preliminaries. We give some background on Gaussian free fields (GFF), SchrammLoewner evolutions (SLE) with force points, conformal loop ensembles (CLE), level lines and twovalued sets of the GFF, the labeled CLE 4 , couplings between the GFF and CLE 4 , the Carathéodory topology, and some useful estimates; in particular, four-arm estimates, estimates for extremal distance, and estimates for the CLE 4 metric.

Section 3: Uniqueness of the metric: genealogy, main result, and proof strategy. In this section, we define a genealogy on the loops of the CLE 4 using only the uniform exploration. Then, we define a distance ˜ D on the CLE 4 that only relies on pieces of geodesics to the boundary. More precisely, the metric ˜ D is obtained by going up and down in the tree of geodesics to the boundary. We then state two key results, namely Propositions 3.4 and 3.5. The first one states that the metric of any geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling coincides a.s. with ˜ D , and the second one shows that the geodesics to the boundary (which we also call the root) are determined by the above-mentioned genealogy: a loop ℒ ( y ) is a descendant of a loop ℒ ( x ) in the genealogy if and only if ℒ ( x ) lies on a geodesic from ∂U to ℒ ( y ). Combined together, Propositions 3.4 and 3.5 entail that any geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling is uniquely determined by the associated uniform exploration (see Theorem 3.1). We then explain the proof strategy in order to obtain Propositions 3.4 and 3.5.

Section 4: Crossings of annuli. We construct an exploration of the crossings of an annulus by CLE 4 loops, and we deduce from it in Lemma 4.1 that these crossings are a.s. determined by the restriction of the GFF to the annulus. We consider the remaining connected components of the annulus, i.e., the components which are left once the crossings of the annulus and the excursions into it from its inner and outer boundaries have been removed. Next, we prove in Lemma 4.7 that, conditionally on these components and with conditional probability at least 1 - 2 - bR , where b &gt; 0 is a universal constant and 2 - R (resp. 1 / 2) is the inner (resp. outer) radius of the annulus, there exist conformal rectangles included in these connected components whose left and right sides are contained in crossings of the annulus made by loops and whose CLE 4 -metric distance from top to bottom is larger than their CLE 4 -metric distance from left to right.

Section 5: Good annuli. We establish that with very high probability, there are many annuli which are good in the sense that they are crossed by exactly two loops of the CLE 4 and that any geodesic from the inner boundary of the annulus to ∂ D has to intersect one of these two loops. The conformal rectangles constructed in the previous section are the main ingredient in the proof of the second property.

Section 6: Covering geodesics by good annuli. This section is devoted to the proof of Propositions 3.4 and 3.5. The main idea is to cover geodesics using the good annuli obtained in the previous section and argue, using these annuli, that geodesics can be approximated by concatenations of pieces of geodesics to the boundary.

Section 7: Measurability. We prove in Proposition 7.1 that any geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling, and hence also the associated uniform exploration, is a measurable function of the CLE 4 . This completes the proof of Theorem 1.5, and it also completes the proofs of Corollary 1.6 and, together with the main results of [KKMT26a, KKMT26b], of Theorem 1.7.

Section 8: Uniqueness of geodesics. We will prove Theorem 1.8, which is restated as Theorem 8.1.

Appendix A: Nested CLE 4 four-arm exponents. We will prove estimates on four-arm events for nested CLE 4 , extending the results in [GNQ25]. These estimates are used in Section 5 to prove Lemma 5.4, which bounds the number of crossings of an annulus by loops of the nested CLE 4 .

## 2. Preliminaries

2.1. Gaussian free fields. We briefly recall some elementary properties of the Gaussian free field (GFF); see [She07] for a review. Let G ⊆ C be a simply connected domain with harmonically non-trivial boundary and let H 1 0 ( G ) denote the Hilbert space closure of C ∞ 0 ( G ) with respect to the Dirichlet inner product defined by setting, for all f, g ∈ C ∞ 0 ( G ),

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

The zero-boundary GFF h is the random distribution defined by

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where ( ϕ n ) n ≥ 1 is a ( · , · ) ∇ -orthonormal basis of H 1 0 ( G ) and ( α n ) n ≥ 1 is a sequence of independent N (0 , 1)-distributed random variables. The law of h does not depend on the choice of orthonormal basis ( ϕ n ) n ≥ 1 .

A GFF on G is not a random function but rather a random variable taking values in the space of distributions (also called generalized functions) on G ; a.s., the series in (2.1) does not converge in H 1 0 ( G ). Nevertheless, the above series converges a.s. in the space of distributions on G . We say that a random distribution h on G is a GFF with boundary conditions given by a function f defined on ∂G if h can be expressed as the sum of a zero-boundary GFF on G and the harmonic extension of f from ∂G to G .

The following are two important properties of the GFF that we will frequently use.

- The Markov property. If h is a zero-boundary GFF on G and V ⊆ G is open, then we can write h = h 0 V + h V , where h 0 V is a zero-boundary GFF on V (that is, independent zero-boundary GFFs, one in each connected component of V ) and h V is a distribution on G which is harmonic on V . Moreover, h 0 V and h V are independent.
- Conformal invariance. If ϕ : G → G ′ is a conformal transformation and h is a GFF on G , then h ◦ ϕ - 1 is a GFF on G ′ .

There is also a variant of the Markov property where one thinks of conditioning on the values of the GFF on a random set, in the same vein as for the strong Markov property and stopping times for Brownian motion. Specifically, a random closed set A ⊆ G coupled with h is said to be local for h [SS13] if we can write h = h 0 A + h A where h A is a distribution on G which is harmonic on G \ A and, given ( A,h A ), the field h 0 A has the law of a GFF on G \ A with zero boundary conditions, independently in each connected component of G \ A .

2.2. Chordal SLE κ ( ρ ) processes. The chordal SLE κ ( ρ ) processes are variants of SLE κ where one keeps track of extra marked points [LSW03]. To define them, we fix κ &gt; 0 and integers ℓ, r ≥ 0, and we write [ a, b ] Z def = [ a, b ] ∩ Z for the set of integers in an interval [ a, b ]. We let x L = ( x ℓ,L , . . . , x 1 ,L ) and x R = ( x 1 ,R , . . . , x r,R ), where x ℓ,L &lt; · · · &lt; x 1 ,L ≤ 0 - and 0 + ≤ x 1 ,R &lt; · · · &lt; x r,R (we allow the innermost force points to be located at 0 - and 0 + , i.e. immediately to the left and to the right of the seed of the curve), and let ρ L = ( ρ 1 ,L , . . . , ρ ℓ,L ) and ρ R = ( ρ 1 ,R , . . . , ρ r,R ), where ρ j,q ∈ R for q ∈ { L, R } and j ∈ [1 , N q ] Z with N L = ℓ , N R = r . Let ( g t ) denote the solution to the ODE

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where W is a solution to

where B is a standard Brownian motion. For each z ∈ H , the solution g t ( z ) to (2.2) is defined up to the time τ ( z ) at which g t ( z ) - W t first hits 0, and we write H t def = { z ∈ H : τ ( z ) &gt; t } for the domain of g t .

For any value of ρ L and ρ R , it is clear that (2.3) has a unique strong solution until the first time t such that W t = V j,q t for some q ∈ { L, R } and j ∈ [1 , N q ] Z . Actually, it was shown in [MS16a, WW17] that, when ∑ k j =1 ρ j,L &gt; - 2 for all k ∈ [1 , ℓ ] Z and ∑ k j =1 ρ j,R &gt; - 2 for all k ∈ [1 , r ] Z , there exists a solution to (2.3) defined for all times t ≥ 0, and for this solution the set of t ≥ 0 for which W t = V j,q t for some q ∈ { L, R } and j ∈ [1 , N q ] Z a.s. has zero Lebesgue measure. The uniqueness in law of such a solution is also proved in [MS16a]. Moreover, it was shown in [MS16a, WW17] that there a.s. exists a continuous curve η such that the domain H t of g t is given by the unbounded connected component of H \ η ([0 , t ]), for all t ≥ 0. The curve η is called a chordal SLE κ ( ρ ) in H from 0 to ∞ . (Processes with ∑ k j =1 ρ j,q ≤ - 2 are not produced by (2.3) and have to be constructed by other means; their continuity was later proved in [MSW17, MS19].)

A chordal SLE κ ( ρ ) process in a proper simply connected domain G ⊆ C is defined as the image of a chordal SLE κ ( ρ ) on H under a conformal transformation mapping H onto G which sends the starting point 0 to the desired starting point on ∂G and the target point ∞ to the desired target point on ∂G . The force points on ∂G are then defined as the images of the corresponding force points on ∂ H . Since the conformal automorphisms of H fi xing 0 and ∞ are the scalings z ↦→ az with a &gt; 0, and the law of a chordal SLE κ ( ρ ) is invariant under such scalings modulo time parameterization, this does not depend on the choice of conformal transformation.

- 2.3. Conformal loop ensembles. Now, we will briefly review CLE and refer the reader to [SW12, MSW17, She09] for more details.

CLE in D is a countable collection Γ of non-nested loops ( γ j ) j ∈ J in D that satisfies the following properties.

- (I) Conformal invariance : For any Möbius transformation ϕ : D → D , the laws of Γ and ϕ (Γ) are the same. This makes it possible to define CLE on any proper simply connected domain G ⊆ C as the image of a CLE on D under a conformal transformation mapping D onto G .
- (II) Domain Markov property : For any non-trivial simply connected domain W ⊆ D , we let W ⋆ be defined as in (1.1), with D , Γ and W in place of U , Γ U and V ; that is, W ⋆ is the set obtained by removing from W the closure of the union of the interiors of the loops of Γ which are not contained in W . Then, conditionally on the loops of Γ which intersect D \ W (which, we note, determines W ⋆ ), the law of Γ restricted to each component V of W ⋆ is that of a CLE in V . Moreover, the restrictions of Γ to distinct components of W ⋆ are independent.

It was shown in [SW12, She09] that for each κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 8) there exists a CLE κ where the loops can be constructed using a branching variant of SLE κ (specifically, a tree built out of a collection of SLE κ ( κ - 6) processes starting from the same point). The loops in the CLE κ are then SLE κ -type curves. From the perspective of the branching tree construction, it is not obvious that the loops of the CLE κ are independent of the choice of root, are continuous curves, and are locally finite. In the case that κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4], this was proved in [SW12] as a consequence of the representation of the CLE κ loops as the outer boundaries of clusters of the Brownian loop-soup; see also [MSW17] for another argument. In the case where κ ∈ (4 , 8), this was proved as a consequence of the continuity of SLE κ ( κ - 6) [MS16a], the reversibility of SLE κ [MS16c], and the continuity of space-filling SLE κ [MS17].

If κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4], we have that CLE κ consists of disjoint simple loops which a.s. do not intersect the domain boundary. However, if κ ∈ (4 , 8), then the loops in the CLE κ are non-simple and can intersect each other and the domain boundary. In the present work, we will focus on the κ = 4 case, where the loops do not intersect each other or the domain boundary but nearly do. It was further shown in [SW12] that if one has an ensemble of loops which satisfies (I) and (II), together with the additional assumption that the loops are simple and do not intersect the boundary or each other, then it must be a CLE κ for some κ ∈ (8 / 3 , 4].

2.4. Level lines of the GFF. Let λ = π/ 2 and let h be an instance of the GFF on H with boundary conditions given by - λ (resp. λ ) on R - (resp. R + ). Then, it was shown in [SS13] that a zero level line η of h from 0 to ∞ can be made sense of in a rigorous way and has the law of a chordal SLE 4 from 0 to ∞ . Moreover, η is characterized by the property that for all t ≥ 0, the conditional law of h given η | [0 ,t ] is that of a GFF on H \ η ([0 , t ]) with boundary conditions given by - λ (resp. λ ) on the left (resp. right) side of η ([0 , t ]) and on R - (resp. R + ).

The results in [SS13] were generalized in [WW17] as follows. Fix a collection of weights ( ρ L ; ρ R ) and force points ( x L ; x R ), and let h be a GFF on H with boundary conditions given by - λ (1 + ∑ j i =0 ρ i,L ) on [ x j +1 ,L , x j,L ) for j ∈ [0 , ℓ ] Z and λ (1+ ∑ j i =0 ρ i,R ) on [ x j,R , x j +1 ,R ) for j ∈ [0 , r ] Z , where ρ 0 ,L = ρ 0 ,R = 0, x 0 ,L = 0 - , x ℓ +1 ,L = -∞ , x 0 ,R = 0 + , and x r +1 ,R = ∞ . Assume moreover that ∑ k j =1 ρ j,L &gt; - 2 for all k ∈ [1 , ℓ ] Z and ∑ k j =1 ρ j,R &gt; - 2 for all k ∈ [1 , r ] Z . Then, the zero level line η of h from 0 to ∞ can be rigorously defined. Moreover, it has the law of a chordal SLE 4 ( ρ L ; ρ R ) and it is a measurable function of h . Furthermore, the level line is characterized by the property that the conditional law of h given η | [0 ,t ] is that of a GFF on H \ η ([0 , t ]) with boundary conditions given by - λ (resp. λ ) on the left (resp. right) side of η ([0 , t ]) and the same boundary values as h on ∂ H .

For all u ∈ R , the level line of h with height u from 0 to ∞ is defined as the level line of h - u from 0 to ∞ . Then, we have the following interaction rules for level lines with different heights proved in [WW17].

Theorem 2.1 ([WW17, Theorems 1.1.4 and 1.1.5]) . Let h be a GFF on H with piecewise constant boundary conditions that change only finitely many times. For all u ∈ R and x ∈ ∂ H , we denote by γ x u the level line of h with height u starting from x and ending at ∞ . Fix x 2 ≤ x 1 and u 1 , u 2 ∈ R .

- (i) If u 2 &lt; u 1 , then γ x 2 u 2 a.s. stays to the left of γ x 1 u 1 .
- (ii) If u 2 = u 1 , then γ x 2 u 2 may intersect γ x 1 u 1 and, upon intersecting, the two curves merge and never separate.
- (iii) If u 1 - u 2 ≥ 2 λ , then γ x 1 u 1 and γ x 2 u 2 do not intersect each other a.s.

2.6. Labeled CLE 4 . Labeled CLE 4 in D , also called the uniform exploration of the CLE 4 on D , was constructed in [WW13] and corresponds to a Markovian exploration of CLE 4 loops, with labels on each loop that keep track of the time when each loop is discovered. We will describe briefly the construction and refer to [WW13] and [WW17] for more details.

To construct the exploration, we define the measure M as the image measure of µ ⊗ ω by the mapping ( ℓ, x ) ↦→ x · ℓ , where ω denotes the harmonic measure on ∂ D as seen from 0, and µ denotes the SLE 4 bubble measure on D pinned at 1 introduced in [SW12]. We also let ( ℓ t ) t ≥ 0 denote a Poisson point process (PPP) with intensity measure given by M times the Lebesgue measure on R + . This process defines an ordering on the (pinned) loops, also often called bubbles, where each loop ℓ is equipped with time label t ℓ .

The exploration targeted at 0 is defined as follows. Let τ 0 denote the first time that the PPP discovers a loop that surrounds 0 and for all ε ∈ (0 , 1), we let ℓ t ε 1 , . . . , ℓ t ε nε denote the loops discovered

until time τ 0 with diameter at least ε , such that t ε 1 &lt; · · · &lt; t ε n ε . We define D 0 def = D and inductively let D i denote the connected component of D i - 1 \ ϕ i ( ℓ t ε i ) containing 0, where ϕ i is the conformal mapping from D onto D i - 1 such that ϕ i (0) = 0 and ϕ ′ i (0) &gt; 0. Then, it was shown in [WW13] that the boundary of D n ε converges as ε → 0 to a loop which has the same law as the loop in a CLE 4 encircling zero. The loop surrounding zero is then equipped with the time label τ 0 and we say that it is discovered at time τ 0 . More generally, if we set D ε t = D i when t ε i - 1 ≤ t &lt; t ε i for all t ≤ τ 0 , where t ε 0 def = 0, then D ε t converges in the Carathéodory sense to a simply connected open domain C t (0), which is called the connected component of the unexplored region containing the origin.

It was shown in [WW13, Lemma 6] that the measure M is invariant under Möbius transformations of D . Therefore, by conformal invariance, the exploration targeted at any fixed point z ∈ D is defined by mapping the exploration targeted at 0 via a Möbius transformation of D which maps 0 to z .

The labeled CLE 4 exploration is then defined by coupling the exploration processes targeted at any two fixed points z, w ∈ D together so that they agree up until the first time that they are separated, and then evolve independently. Each loop ℒ of the CLE 4 can then be labeled by the time t ℒ at which it is discovered. For all z ∈ D , we denote by C t ( z ) the connected component of the unexplored region containing z . The explored region is the compact set

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

We use here the same symbol ℬ t as for the metric balls of Definition 1.3. Let us emphasize that this is not an assumption: that the explored region at time t coincides with the metric ball of radius t from ∂ D is precisely what Axiom (IV) of Definition 1.3 asserts. Since every rational point is encircled by a loop and since C t ( z ) is open, we can also write C t ( z ) = ⋃ x ∈ C t ( z ) ∩ Q 2 int( ℒ ( x )).

- 2.7. Two-valued sets of the GFF. Next, we introduce the two-valued (local) sets of a GFF h on D that we are going to consider. Recall from Section 2.1 the definition of a local set of h , introduced in [SS13]. We refer to [ASW19] and [AS18] for more detailed expositions of two-valued sets of the GFF.

Definition 2.2. Let a, b &gt; 0 and let h be a GFF in a proper simply connected domain G ⊆ C . We say that a local set A of h is a two-valued local set of h of levels - a and b if it satisfies the following properties.

- (i) If h A denotes the harmonic extension to G \ A of the boundary values of h on A , then we have that h A ∈ {- a, b } a.s.
- (ii) For any smooth test function f ∈ C ∞ 0 ( G ), a.s.,

- (iii) The set A ∪ ∂G has a finite number of connected components.

Suppose that h has zero boundary conditions. It was shown in [ASW19] that if a + b ≥ 2 λ , then such a set exists and it is a.s. determined by h ; we denote it by A - a,b , and by A - a,b ( O ) when we wish to record the domain O in which it is constructed. Moreover, A - a,b is unique in the sense that if A ′ is another local set of h satisfying (i), (ii) and (iii), then A ′ = A - a,b a.s.

Let us now briefly review the construction of A - a, - a +2 λ for a ∈ (0 , 2 λ ), which will be needed for our purposes. We follow [AS18, Section 3.2].

To begin with, we let η denote the level line of h + a - λ from - i to i and set A 1 def = η ([0 , ∞ ]). In each connected component O of D \ A 1 lying to the left of η , we let x and y be the two endpoints of the arc ∂O ∩ ∂ D , and we assume that ( - i , x, y ) are ordered in the counterclockwise way. We then let η O denote the level line of h | O + a - λ from x to y .

As for the connected components O of D \ A 1 lying to the right of η , we perform an analogous procedure except for exploring the level line of h | O + a - λ from y to x . We also denote those level lines by η O .

̸

In the next step of the exploration, we let A 2 be the closure of the union of A 1 with the η O 's for each connected component O of D \ A 1 . In the connected components of D \ A 2 whose boundaries are contained in A 2 , we stop the exploration. Note that the boundary conditions of h on each of the aforementioned components are a.s. constant and given by either - a or 2 λ - a . In the rest of the components, we iterate as before to obtain A n . We note that if O is a connected component of D \ A 2 such that ∂O ∩ ∂ D = ∅ , then we have that the boundary conditions of h | O are given by 0 on ∂O ∩ ∂ D , and - a (resp. 2 λ - a ) on ∂O \ ∂ D if O lies to the left (resp. right) of A 1 . Then, we set A - a, - a +2 λ def = ⋃ n ∈ N A n .

Remark 2.3 . The uniqueness of the two-valued set A - a,b implies that the starting and ending points of the level lines and the order in which they were sampled to produce A - a,b do not matter. This boils down to the reversibility of the SLE 4 ( ρ ) processes.

Remark 2.4 . The set A - a, - a +2 λ constructed above is equal to the range of a clockwise boundary conformal loop ensemble with κ = 4 and parameter ρ (i.e., a BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ) process) with ρ = - a/λ introduced in [MSW17, Section 7]. Recall that the loops in the complement of the BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ) with a clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) orientation are its 'true' (resp. false) loops and the true loops intersect the domain boundary as an SLE 4 ( ρ ) process. By varying a ∈ (0 , 2 λ ), we obtain BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ) for all ρ ∈ ( - 2 , 0). The loops with boundary condition 2 λ - a in A - a, - a +2 λ correspond to the true loops of the BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ) and the loops with boundary condition - a in A - a, - a +2 λ correspond to the false loops of the BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ).

Fix r ∈ (0 , 2 λ ). For all j ∈ N , we define sets B j r iteratively as follows. First, we set B 1 r def = A - r, - r +2 λ . In each connected component O of D \ B 1 r such that the boundary conditions of h | O are given by - r , we explore the set A - r, - r +2 λ ( O ) which is defined in the same way as A - r, - r +2 λ , but with the zero-boundary GFF h | O - h B 1 r in place of h , where we recall that h B 1 r is the harmonic extension to D \ B 1 r of the boundary values of h on B 1 r . Then, we let B 2 r denote the closure of the union of the sets explored. Note that the boundary conditions of h on the connected components of D \ B 2 r are constant and lie in { 2 λ - r, 2 λ - 2 r, - 2 r } .

Suppose that we have constructed B j r for j ∈ N . Then, in each connected component O of D \ B j r such that the boundary conditions of h | O are given by - jr , we explore the set A - r, - r +2 λ ( O ) defined in the same way as A - r, - r +2 λ but with the zero-boundary GFF h | O - h B j r in place of h . We define B j +1 r as the closure of the union of B j r and the sets explored.

We set B r def = ⋃ j ∈ N B j r and B 0 def = ⋃ n ∈ N B 2 - n . To every loop ℓ of B 0 (i.e., whose interior int( ℓ ) is a connected component of D \ B 0 ), we associate a label t ℓ such that the boundary conditions of h | int( ℓ ) are given by 2 λ - t ℓ . Then, we have the following.

Proposition 2.5 ([AS18, Proposition 6.6]) . The collection of loops in B 0 has the law of a nonnested CLE 4 Γ on D and { ( ℓ, t ℓ ) } ℓ ∈ Γ has the law of a labeled CLE 4 .

Besides, let us recall from [KKMT26a] that for all r ∈ (0 , 2 λ ), one can couple ( ℬ s ( ∂ D )) s ≥ 0 with a BCLE ⟳ 4 ( - r/λ ) so that all the domains encircled by false (counterclockwise) loops of the BCLE ⟳ 4 ( - r/λ ) are exactly the connected components of the unexplored region D \ ℬ r ( ∂ D ) whose boundary intersects ∂ D . More precisely, we proved the following result.

Lemma 2.6 ([KKMT26a, Lemma 2.9]) . Consider the uniform exploration ( ℬ s ( ∂ D )) s ≥ 0 , let r ∈ (0 , 2 λ ) and consider the BCLE ⟳ 4 ( - r/λ ) coupled with ( ℬ s ( ∂ D )) s ≥ 0 as above. Then, the interiors of the false BCLE ⟳ 4 ( - r/λ ) loops are exactly the connected components of D \ ℬ r ( ∂ D ) whose boundary intersects ∂ D .

- 2.9. Four-arm estimates. Next, we mention one useful four-arm estimate for bi-chordal SLE 4 which we are going to use in the last section. It estimates the probability that both of the two branches of the bi-chordal SLE 4 intersect a small Euclidean ball centered at an interior point of the domain where the bi-chordal SLE 4 is defined. Lemma 2.7 follows immediately from [Zha20, Theorem 1.1].

Lemma 2.7. Let z 1 , z 2 , z 3 , z 4 be points on ∂ D ordered in the counterclockwise way. Let also { η 12 , η 34 } be a bi-chordal SLE 4 in D with (interior) link pattern {{ z 1 , z 2 } , { z 3 , z 4 }} . Then, there exists a universal constant C ∈ (0 , ∞ ) such that the following holds for all ε ∈ (0 , 1) . The probability that both η 12 and η 34 intersect B ε (0) is at most Cε 2 .

Proof. The claim in the statement of the lemma follows from [Zha20, Theorem 1.1]. □

- 2.11. Locality property for metric balls. In the present subsection, we record the fact that metric balls satisfy a locality property which is a consequence of the results from [KKMT26b]. Roughly speaking, this means that conditionally on a metric ball, the loop configuration in each complementary component is a non-nested CLE 4 , and the internal metric on that component is precisely the metric associated with this CLE 4 .

Then the following is true: Let V ⊂ U be a deterministic simply connected domain. Write { V j } j for the connected components of V ⋆ (recall (1.1) for the definition of V ⋆ ). The same is true with a deterministic connected arc of ∂U in place of ℒ ( z ) .

## 3. Uniqueness of the metric: genealogy, main result, and proof strategy

In this section and in Sections 4, 5 and 6, we will show that any geodesic CLE 4 metric can be recovered from the uniform exploration. This result is the key ingredient toward proving Theorem 1.5. Let us state this result more precisely.

Theorem 3.1. Let U be a simply connected domain and ( ℬ t ( ∂U )) t ≥ 0 be a uniform exploration of Γ U from the boundary. Let D be a geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling and assume that for all t ≥ 0 ,

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

Then, the distance D U Γ U is a uniquely defined measurable function of ( ℬ t ( ∂U )) t ≥ 0 .

In particular, the above result already shows that the geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling is unique in law. To prove Theorem 3.1, we first define a genealogy of loops using the uniform exploration. Then, we prove that the geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling is uniquely determined by the distances along geodesics to the boundary and we conclude by showing that the geodesics to the boundary are indeed described by the genealogy of loops. By conformal invariance, we focus on U = D .

- 3.1. Definition of a genealogy from the uniform exploration. Let D be a geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling. Consider a loop ℒ ( x ) of the CLE 4 discovered at some time t x ≥ 0 (note that a.s. t x &gt; 0, since the loops of a CLE 4 do not touch ∂ D ) by the uniform exploration ( ℬ t ( ∂ D ; D D Γ D )) t ≥ 0 . Let V be the connected component of D \ ℬ t x ( ∂ D ; D D Γ D ) such that ℒ ( x ) ⊆ ∂V . Note that such a component exists and is unique.

Let t x &lt; t 1 &lt; · · · &lt; t k for k ≥ 1 . The set ℬ t 1 ( ∂ D ; D D Γ D ) ∩ V has countably many connected components, which are denoted by ( C 1 ,j ) j ≥ 1 (with the convention C 1 ,j = ∅ if there are less than j connected components). We will call these annuli that satisfy conditions (iii) and (iv) good . The first step will be to prove that conditions (iii) and (iv) hold with high probability for the annulus B 1 / 2 ( z ) \ B 2 - R ( z ) provided that R &gt; 1 is large. In particular, we will show in Lemma 5.1 that the above probability is at least 1 - 2 - αR for some universal constant α &gt; 2.

The second step will be to use the independence across scales property of the GFF (see [GM20, Lemma 3.1]) to argue that for fixed z ∈ D , we have off an event whose probability decays to zero as K →∞ exponentially in K , that there exists 1 ≤ k ≤ K such that the annulus B 2 - k ( z ) \ B 2 - k - R ( z ) satisfies conditions (iii) and (iv) (see Lemma 5.3). Then, we can take a union bound over all z lying in a suitably dense grid of points. However, in order to be able to apply the independence across scales property argument, we need to generate the crossings of annuli by loops in Γ in a way which is local with respect to some suitable GFF on D . For that purpose, we are going to use a coupling (Ψ , Γ) between a zero-boundary GFF Ψ on D and Γ described e.g. in [ASW19]. In particular, we will show that for a fixed annulus A ⊆ D , the collection of crossings of A made by loops in Γ is a.s. determined by the restriction of Ψ to A (see Lemma 4.1).

That condition (iii) holds with high probability will follow from two main estimates. The first estimate states that there exists a universal constant b &gt; 2 such that for R &gt; 1 sufficiently large, we have off an event with probability at most 2 - bR that there are at most four crossings of B 1 / 2 (0) \ B 2 - R (0) made by loops in Γ (see Lemma 5.4). The second estimate roughly states that if A ⊆ D is an annulus of size 2 - R and we grow two metric balls with respect to D Γ starting from two distinct loops in Γ and stopped at the first time that they intersect A , then it is very likely that their D Γ -distance is very small (see Lemma 6.2 and Lemma 6.1). This will imply that there have to be at least two loops in Γ crossing A since otherwise, the D Γ -distance between the inner and outer boundaries of A would not be sufficiently small. Combining the two estimates then yields condition (iii): a loop which crosses A does so at least twice, so the bound of at most four crossings together with the presence of at least two crossing loops forces there to be exactly two such loops, each crossing A exactly twice.

That condition (iv) holds with sufficiently high probability will follow from a more complicated argument (see Lemmas 4.7 and 6.5). The argument goes roughly as follows. Fix R &gt; 1 large and consider the annulus A def = B 1 / 2 (0) \ B 2 - R (0). Let Γ out A (resp. Γ in A ) denote the collection of segments of loops in Γ which make an excursion from the outer (resp. inner) boundary of A . Let also Γ A denote the collection of loops in Γ which cross A . Suppose that we are working on the event that the region bounded by the loop in Γ containing 0 does not contain B 1 / 2 (0), and let U 1 , . . . , U N denote the connected components of the set

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

We will show in Lemma 4.7 that there exists a universal constant b &gt; 0 such that for all sufficiently large R &gt; 1, the following holds. Conditional on U 1 , . . . , U N and on the event that the field Ψ | U j has zero boundary conditions for a fixed j ∈ { 1 , . . . , N } , there exist, with conditional probability at least 1 - 2 - bR , conformal rectangles R j,m for 1 ≤ m ≤ 8 satisfying the following properties:

- (v) Each conformal rectangle R j,m is contained in U j , and its left (resp. right) boundary is contained in a crossing of A made by a loop in Γ.
- (vi) No loop in Γ crosses R j,m from its top to its bottom boundary, and the D Γ -distance between the left and right sides of R j,m is strictly less than the D Γ -distance between its top and bottom sides.

Suppose that conditions (v) and (vi) hold. If P is a D Γ -geodesic crossing A which does not intersect any loop of Γ crossing A , then there must exist j ∈ { 1 , . . . , N } such that P crosses the conformal rectangle R j,m from its top to its bottom side for all m = 1 , . . . , 8. However, condition (vi) implies that we can replace this segment of P with another admissible path having the same endpoints but strictly smaller D Γ -length. Indeed, we can replace each top-to-bottom crossing I of R j,m made by P with the concatenation of a subsegment of I and a D Γ -geodesic connecting the left and right sides of R j,m . Doing this for all 1 ≤ m ≤ 8 allows us to replace the portion of P crossing all eight rectangles with an admissible path having the same endpoints but strictly smaller D Γ -length. This contradicts the geodesic property of P . It follows that P must intersect a loop in Γ which crosses A .

## 4. Crossings of annuli

Throughout this section, we let Ψ be a zero-boundary GFF on D and let Γ be a nested CLE 4 on D such that the pair (Ψ , Γ) is coupled as in Section 2.5. We also let Γ denote the collection of outermost loops in Γ, and we fix R &gt; 1 and let A def = B 1 / 2 (0) \ B 2 - R (0) denote the corresponding annulus. Our goal in the present section is to study the collection of segments of loops in Γ which are contained in A , except for their two endpoints, which lie on ∂A . First we will prove the following measurability statement. We say that a loop ℒ in Γ has height (2 k + 1) λ , where k ∈ Z , if the boundary values of Ψ on one side of ℒ are equal to 2 kλ and on the other side are equal to (2 k +2) λ . The height of a segment of a loop is by definition the height of the loop of which it is a part.

Lemma 4.1. The collection of segments of loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } which are contained in A , except for their two endpoints, which lie on ∂A , is a.s. determined by the restriction of the field Ψ to A .

Before we prove Lemma 4.1, we will give a brief overview of its proof and introduce the setup that we are going to use.

The main idea of the proof of Lemma 4.1 is the following. First we will construct a deterministic, countable collection of conformal rectangles ( ℛ n ) n ∈ N satisfying the following property. Let ℒ : [0 , 1] → A be a segment of a loop of Γ with height in {- λ, λ } such that { ℒ (0) , ℒ (1) } ⊆ ∂A and ℒ ((0 , 1)) ⊆ A . Then for all 0 &lt; s &lt; t &lt; 1, there exists n ∈ N such that ℒ ([ s, t ]) is part of a crossing of ℛ n ∩ A of height in {- λ, λ } from its top to its bottom boundary. Next we will use the exploration procedure introduced in [KMS22, Section 5], which recovers the crossings of ℛ n ∩ A in a way which is measurable with respect to Ψ | ℛ n ∩ A . Since s and t were arbitrary, letting s ↓ 0 and t ↑ 1 we obtain that ℒ is a.s. determined by Ψ | A .

4.1. Constructing tubes containing the crossings. Now we proceed with the construction of the conformal rectangles mentioned above. Fix distinct points x, y ∈ ∂ D and 0 &lt; δ &lt; 2 - R - 2 | x - y | . Let x L δ be the point on the clockwise arc of ∂ D from x to y such that | x L δ - x | = δ and let x R δ be the point on the counterclockwise arc of ∂ D from x to y such that | x R δ - x | = δ . Similarly, we let y L δ be the point on the clockwise arc of ∂ D from y to x such that | y L δ - y | = δ and let y R δ be the point on the counterclockwise arc of ∂ D from y to x such that | y R δ - y | = δ ; each of these four points is uniquely determined since δ &lt; | x - y | / 2.

Moreover, we define simple curves ˜ ℒ L δ , ˜ ℒ R δ as follows. First we let I L δ ( x ) (resp. I R δ ( x )) be the segment starting from x L δ (resp. x R δ ) which is parallel to [ x, 0] and points toward the interior of D , stopped at the first time that it hits ∂B 1 / 2 - δ (0) (resp. ∂B 2 - R + δ (0)). Also we let I L δ ( y ) (resp. I R δ ( y )) be the segment starting from y L δ (resp. y R δ ) which is parallel to [ y, 0] and points toward the interior of D , stopped at the first time that it hits ∂B 2 - R + δ (0) (resp. ∂B 1 / 2 - δ (0)). Note that the Euclidean distance from I L δ ( x ) and I R δ ( x ) to [ x, 0] (resp. from I L δ ( y ) and I R δ ( y ) to [ y, 0]) is equal to δ √ 1 - δ 2 / 4 ≤ δ . Then the curve ˜ ℒ L δ follows I L δ ( x ) up until it hits ∂B 1 / 2 - δ (0), then follows ∂B 1 / 2 - δ (0) in the clockwise direction up until it hits I R δ ( y ), and finally it follows I R δ ( y ) up until it hits y R δ . Similarly the curve ˜ ℒ R δ follows I R δ ( x ) up until it hits ∂B 2 - R + δ (0), then follows ∂B 2 - R + δ (0) in the clockwise direction up until it hits I L δ ( y ), and finally it follows I L δ ( y ) up until it hits y L δ . The assumption that δ &lt; 2 - R - 2 | x - y | ensures that ˜ ℒ L δ and ˜ ℒ R δ are indeed simple and that they are disjoint. Let G be the conformal rectangle bounded by ˜ ℒ L δ , ˜ ℒ R δ , the clockwise arc of ∂ D from x R δ to x L δ , and the counterclockwise arc of ∂ D from y L δ to y R δ , with the four marked boundary points x R δ , x L δ , y R δ , y L δ listed in cyclic order. See the left-hand side of Figure 4. The countable collection of conformal rectangles ( ℛ n ) n ∈ N referred to above consists of the tubes G obtained in this way as x, y range over a fixed countable dense subset of ∂ D and δ ranges over the rational numbers in (0 , 2 - R - 2 | x - y | ).

4.2. Exploration of the crossings. Let ϕ be the unique conformal map from G onto the rectangle R def =(0 , 1) × (0 , H ), where H ∈ (0 , ∞ ), such that ϕ ( x R δ ) = 0 , ϕ ( x L δ ) = i H,ϕ ( y R δ ) = 1 + i H , and ϕ ( y L δ ) = 1. Fix ξ ∈ (0 , H/ 10) and set

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

We will perform a level line exploration of the field Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 - λ in the rectangles R D , R U from their left to their right boundaries. Here we recall that, conditionally on Ψ | D \ G , the field Ψ | G is a GFF on G whose boundary values are equal to 0 on the two arcs of ∂G which are contained in ∂ D , that is, on the two sides of G which are mapped by ϕ to the left and to the right side of R . The purpose of the exploration is to generate the crossings of A in a way which is measurable with respect to Ψ. The exploration is very similar to the level line exploration introduced in [KMS22, Section 5].

Now we proceed with the description of the exploration. We fix u ∈ ( - 2 λ, 0) with 2 λ/u ∈ N (e.g., u def = - λ ) and let η 1 be the level line of Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 - λ of height u , i.e. the level line of the field Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 - λ - u , started from the midpoint of the left boundary of R D (resp. R U ). The choice of u ∈ ( - 2 λ, 0) guarantees that the level line η 1 is well-defined (in the sense that the weights of the associated force points are larger than - 2) and that η 1 intersects the parts of ∂R D (resp. ∂R U ) immediately to the left and to the right of its starting point (see [MW17, Lemma 2.1]). If η 1 exits R D (resp. R U ) on its right boundary, then the whole procedure terminates, as explained below. Otherwise there are two possible outcomes:

- (i) η 1 exits R D (resp. R U ) on its bottom boundary. If this occurs, then we start to explore the level line ˜ η 1 of - Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 + λ + u from the point of the left boundary of R D (resp. R U ) with the largest imaginary part among the points which have been visited by the exploration. If ˜ η 1 hits the top boundary of R D (resp. R U ) before hitting either its right or bottom boundary, then the first stage of the exploration is concluded (as depicted on the right-hand side of Figure 4) and we then iterate the exploration as described below. If ˜ η 1 hits the bottom boundary of R D (resp. R U ) before hitting either its right or its top boundary, then we explore the level line of Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 - λ of height u from the point of the left boundary of R D (resp. R U ) with the largest imaginary part among the points which have been visited by the exploration at this point in time. We repeat this step until the newly generated level line of height u hits the top boundary of R D (resp. R U ) before hitting either its bottom or its right boundary, which concludes the first stage of the exploration.
- (ii) η 1 exits R D (resp. R U ) on its top boundary. If this occurs, then we start to explore the level line ˜ η 1 of - Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 + λ + u from the point on the left boundary of R D (resp. R U ) which has the smallest imaginary part among the points which have been visited by the exploration. If ˜ η 1 hits the bottom boundary of R D (resp. R U ) before hitting either its right or its top boundary, then the first stage of the exploration is concluded and next we iterate the exploration as described below. If ˜ η 1 hits the top boundary of R D (resp. R U ) before hitting either the bottom or right boundary, then we explore the level line of Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 - λ of height u from the point of the left boundary of R D (resp. R U ) which has the smallest imaginary part among the points which have been visited by the exploration at this point in time. We repeat this step until the newly generated level line of height u hits the bottom boundary of R D (resp. R U ) before hitting either its top or its right boundary, concluding the first stage of the exploration.

We note that if we discover a level line which hits the right boundary of R D (resp. R U ) in the procedure described above, then the entire procedure terminates.

Let K 1 be the union of the left boundary of R D (resp. R U ) and the set discovered by the first stage of the exploration. If the procedure has not been terminated, we let U 1 be the connected component of R D \ K 1 (resp. R U \ K 1 ) which has the right boundary of R D (resp. R U ) as part of its boundary and we let L 1 = ∂U 1 \ ∂R D (resp. ∂U 1 \ ∂R U ).

We note that L 1 consists of two level lines, one of Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 - λ - u and one of - Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 + λ + u , which both start from the left boundary of R D (resp. R U ) and one of which exits the rectangle on its top boundary while the other exits on its bottom boundary. This implies that the boundary conditions of Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 - λ along L 1 are constant and equal to either - λ + u or λ + u . We say that L 1 is a crossing of R D (resp. R U ) from its top to its bottom boundary of height u , where heights are measured with respect to the field Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 - λ . Equivalently, the boundary values of Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 on the two sides of L 1 are equal to u and to 2 λ + u .

We define the successive stages of the exploration inductively as follows. Suppose that j stages have been completed and that the exploration has not yet discovered a level line which hits the right boundary of R D (resp. R U ). Let K j be the union of the left boundary of R D (resp. R U ) and the set discovered by the exploration after its first j stages, let U j be the connected component of R D \ K j (resp. R U \ K j ) which has the right boundary of R D (resp. R U ) as part of its boundary, and let L j def = ∂U j \ ∂R D (resp. ∂U j \ ∂R U ) be the j th crossing and u j its height. For the iteration step, we make the following definition.

- (i) If the boundary conditions of Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 - λ on L j are equal to λ + u j , then we let η j +1 be the level line of Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 - λ of height u j +1 = u j + u starting from the rightmost point of L j with imaginary part equal to 5 ξ/ 2 (resp. H - 5 ξ/ 2).
- (ii) If the boundary conditions of Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 - λ on L j are equal to - λ + u j , then we let η j +1 be the level line of Ψ | G ◦ ϕ - 1 - λ of height u j +1 = u j - u starting from the rightmost point on L j with imaginary part equal to 5 ξ/ 2 (resp. H - 5 ξ/ 2).

̸

Then we repeat the steps described in the first stage of the exploration by replacing R D (resp. R U ) by U j , the left boundary of R D (resp. R U ) by L j , and η 1 by η j +1 as follows. In the choice of where to start the next level line, we replace the point of intersection with the largest (resp. smallest) imaginary part by the last point with respect to the upward (resp. downward) exploration of L j which was intersected by the previous level line. If any of these level lines exits the rectangle R D (resp. R U ) on its right boundary, then we stop the exploration at that point. From this exploration, we obtain a sequence of crossings ( L j ) j of R D (resp. R U ) from its top to its bottom boundary with the property that L j ∩ L j +1 = ∅ for each j . Note that so that the crossing L j is part of a loop of Γ precisely when u j ∈ 2 λ Z . Moreover, we have the following.

Lemma 4.2. The explorations in R D and in R U both terminate after finitely many stages a.s.

Proof. The statement of the lemma follows from the same argument as the one used to prove [KMS22, Lemma 5.6]. □

For q ∈ { D , U } , we let ˜ η q i for i = 1 , . . . , n q be the crossings of R q with height in {- λ, λ } which are discovered by the exploration, ordered from left to right, and write ˜ η q 0 (resp. ˜ η q n q +1 ) for the left (resp. right) side of R q . Note that each of these crossings will continue into R I and either form a crossing of R I or make an excursion into R I which returns to R q . We let ˜ η 1 , . . . , ˜ η ˜ n be the aforementioned crossings of R I , ordered from left to right, ˜ η ˜ n +2 , . . . , ˜ η ˜ n + ˜ m +1 be the level line excursions into R I from either R D or R U which return to the rectangle from which they started, with height in {- λ, λ } , and let ˜ η 0 (resp. ˜ η n +1 ) be the left (resp. right) side of R I .

Now we are ready to prove Lemma 4.1.

Proof of Lemma 4.1. Suppose that we have the setup described in the previous paragraphs. First we note that the level line interaction rules (see [WW17, Theorem 1.1.4]) imply that any segment of a loop in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } which either crosses ϕ - 1 ( R I ) from its top to its bottom boundary or makes an excursion into ϕ - 1 ( R I ) from one of these two boundaries back to that same boundary

˜

has to be one of the level line segments ϕ - 1 ( ˜ η 1 ) , . . . , ϕ - 1 ( ˜ η ˜ n ) , ϕ - 1 ( ˜ η ˜ n +2 ) , . . . , ϕ - 1 ( ˜ η ˜ n + ˜ m +1 ). Moreover, since level lines are locally determined by the field (see [WW17, Lemma 2.3.15]), we obtain that the sub-collection of those segments of loops in Γ which are contained in A is a.s. determined by Ψ | A . Note that by letting ξ → 0 and δ → 0 along countable sequences and then varying x, y over a fixed countable and dense subset of ∂ D , we can recover all of the segments of loops in Γ which stay in A . Combining the above completes the proof of the lemma. □

Next we let Γ out A (resp. Γ in A ) be the collection of segments of loops in Γ (i.e., outermost loops in Γ) with height in {- λ, λ } which make an excursion into A from the outer (resp. inner) boundary of A . Let also Γ A denote the collection of segments of loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } which cross A . Given a loop ℒ of Γ, we write int( ℒ ) for the region enclosed by ℒ . Then the local finiteness of Γ (see [SW12]) implies that the complement in A of the set

has finitely many connected components which we denote by U 1 , . . . , U N .

We will be interested in those U j which are not surrounded by any loop in Γ and which are such that ∂U j contains a crossing of A made by a loop of Γ with height in {± λ } . First we will show the following.

Lemma 4.3. Fix j ∈ { 1 , . . . , N } . If

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

then we have that U j is surrounded by an outermost loop in Γ .

Proof. First we note that the construction of the coupling between Ψ and Γ explained in Section 2.5 implies that the outermost loops in Γ have height in {- λ, λ } . Moreover for every fixed point z ∈ D , we have that it is a.s. the case that there exists an outermost loop ℓ in Γ such that ℓ surrounds z . Applying this to each point of a fixed countable dense subset of ∂A , we obtain that it is a.s. the case that every such point is surrounded by an outermost loop in Γ. Suppose now that z is a point of ∂U j which does not lie in ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ out A ∪ Γ in A ∪ Γ A ℒ . Then z ∈ ∂A , and since ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ out A ∪ Γ in A ∪ Γ A ℒ is closed, the same is true for all of the points of ∂A in a neighborhood of z ; in particular, we can take z to belong to the countable dense set fixed above. Therefore there exists a loop ℓ in Γ of height in {- λ, λ } such that z is surrounded by ℓ . But then the region which is enclosed by ℓ has to intersect U j . Since ℓ ∩ U j = ∅ by the definition of U j , we obtain that U j is contained in the region which is enclosed by ℓ . In particular, we have that U j is surrounded by an outermost loop in Γ and so this completes the proof of the lemma. □

Lemma 4.3 implies that we will only be interested in connected components whose boundaries are contained in the closure of the union of the segments of loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } . Next we will show that if U j is a connected component as above such that

and the boundary conditions of Ψ | U j on ∂U j are not given by the zero function, then U j is surrounded by an outermost loop in Γ.

Lemma 4.4. Fix j ∈ { 1 , . . . , N } such that

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

and such that ∂U j contains a crossing of A made by a loop of Γ with height in {± λ } . If the boundary conditions of the field Ψ | U j on ∂U j are not given by the zero function, then we have that U j is contained in the region enclosed by an outermost loop in Γ .

Proof. Suppose that the assumptions in the statement of the lemma hold. Then the set ∂U j contains exactly two arcs I 1 j and I 2 j which both belong to Γ A . We have the following two cases.

Step 1. The case where the boundary conditions of Ψ | U j on ∂U j \ ( I 1 j ∪ I 2 j ) are not equal to zero. In that case, there exists a segment of a loop ℒ in Γ out A ∪ Γ in A such that ℒ ⊆ ∂U j and the boundary conditions of Ψ | U j on ℒ are non-zero. Since ℒ is part of some loop ℓ in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } , we obtain that the boundary conditions of Ψ | U j on ℒ are constant and given by either 2 λ or - 2 λ (recall also the coupling between Ψ and Γ described in Section 2.5).

̸

If U j ∩ int( ℓ ) = ∅ , then the construction of U j implies that U j ⊆ int( ℓ ), where we recall that int( ℓ ) denotes the region enclosed by ℓ . Thus the component U j is surrounded by an outermost loop in Γ. If U j ∩ int( ℓ ) = ∅ , then the boundary condition of Ψ | U j on ℓ is non-zero. This means that ℓ cannot be an outermost loop. So there exists a loop ℓ in Γ which is an outermost loop and surrounds ℓ . Then it follows that U j ∩ int( ℓ ) = ∅ and so ℓ surrounds U j .

̸

Step 2. The case where the boundary conditions of Ψ | U j on I 1 j ∪ I 2 j are not equal to zero. Let ℓ 1 j (resp. ℓ 2 j ) be the loop in Γ containing I 1 j (resp. I 2 j ). Then the boundary conditions of Ψ | U j are equal to 2 λ or to - 2 λ on at least one of I 1 j and I 2 j . Without loss of generality, we can assume that the former case holds since a similar argument will work for the latter case. Moreover we order the crossings of A made by loops in Γ in counterclockwise order starting from I 1 j and assume without loss of generality that I 2 j lies to the right of I 1 j .

Suppose that the boundary conditions of Ψ | int( ℓ 1 j ) on ℓ 1 j are given by zero. Then the loop ℓ 1 j is not an outermost loop and so we let ℓ 1 j be the outermost loop in Γ surrounding ℓ 1 j . If ℓ 1 j does not surround ℓ 2 j , we must have that ℓ 1 j separates I 1 j from I 2 j and so ℓ 1 j makes a crossing of A lying between I 1 j and I 2 j . But the crossings made by ℓ 1 j have height in {- λ, λ } and so we obtain a contradiction since there are no crossings in Γ A intersecting U j . Hence we must have that ℓ 1 j surrounds ℓ 2 j and so U j ⊆ int( ℓ 1 j ).

̸

Suppose that the boundary conditions of Ψ | int( ℓ 1 j ) on ℓ 1 j are given by either 2 λ or - 2 λ . Then the right side of I 1 j (when seen as a collection of prime ends in ∂U j ) is contained in ∂ int( ℓ 1 j ), which implies that U j ∩ int( ℓ 1 j ) = ∅ . Hence we obtain that U j is surrounded by ℓ 1 j .

Combining everything, we obtain that U j is surrounded by an outermost loop in Γ in both cases and so this completes the proof of the lemma. □

On the event that there are no crossings of A by loops of Γ with height in {- λ, λ } , for all γ ∈ P A , we denote by ̂ Γ γ the collection of loops in Γ wih height in {- λ, λ } which intersect ∂A ∪ γ . - 4.3. Constructing conformal rectangles. In the present subsection, we are going to construct pairwise disjoint conformal rectangles which are contained in the components U j for j ∈ { 1 , . . . , N } introduced in Section 4.2 for which the field Ψ | U j has zero-boundary conditions, and such that the conformal rectangles satisfy properties (v) and (vi) introduced in Section 3.2. In particular, we will prove the following result.

Lemma 4.7. Suppose that we have the same setup as in Remarks 4.5 and 4.6, where we explore crossings of the nested CLE 4 Γ on D with height in {- λ, λ } . Then, there exist universal constants b &gt; 0 , R 0 &gt; 0 , and B &gt; 0 , such that the following is true for all R ≥ R 0 . Conditional on U 1 , . . . , U N , the following holds with conditional probability at least 1 - 2 - bR . Fix j ∈ { 1 , . . . , N } such that the boundary conditions of Ψ | U j on ∂U j are given by the zero function and such that ∂U j contains a crossing of A made by a loop of Γ with height in {± λ } . Suppose also that we are working on the event that I 3 j ∩ ∂B 2 - 2 R/ 3 (0) = ∅ and I 4 j ∩ ∂B 2 - R/ 3 (0) = ∅ . Then, there exists a collection of conformal rectangles R j,m contained in U j for 1 ≤ m ≤ 8 , satisfying the following properties (see Figure 6 for an illustration).

- (i) R j,m +1 disconnects R j,m from I 3 j for all m = 1 , 2 , . . . , 7 in the following sense. Any path in U j which connects I 3 j with I 4 j and intersects R j,m , has to cross R j,m +1 from its top boundary to its bottom boundary. Moreover, the left (resp. right) boundary of R j,m is contained in I 1 j (resp. I 2 j ).
- (ii) For 1 ≤ m ≤ 8 , we let Γ j,m denote the collection of outermost loops in Γ | U j with height in {- λ, λ } which intersect ∂R j,m . Then, there are no loops in Γ j,m intersecting both the top and bottom marked boundary arcs of ∂R j,m and the D U j,m Γ | U j,m -distance between the left / right marked boundary arcs of ∂U j,m is strictly less than B and the D U j,m Γ | U j,m -distance between the top / bottom marked boundary arcs of ∂U j,m is at least B , where U j,m denotes the connected component of R j,m \ ⋃ ℒ ∈ Γ j,m ℒ whose boundary intersects both the left and right marked boundary arcs of R j,m . For later use, we also require the following more precise properties. Here and below, given a conformal rectangle W with four marked boundary arcs, we write L ( W ) (resp. R ( W ) ) for its left (resp. right) marked boundary arc. One can divide the rectangle U j,m from top to bottom into three conformal rectangles U 1 j,m , U 2 j,m , U 3 j,m as in Figure 6 such that
- (a) The D U j,m Γ | U j,m -distance from the left boundary L ( U 2 j,m ) of U 2 j,m to the right boundary of U 2 j,m is less than B ;
- (b) The right boundary (as defined in Lemma 2.13) of the metric ball ℬ B ( L ( U j,m ) , D U j,m Γ | U j,m ) intersects the left boundary L ( U 2 j,m ) ;
- (c) The D U j,m Γ | U j,m -distance from the bottom side to the top side of U 1 j,m (resp. U 3 j,m ) is at least B .

Moreover, in the case that there are no crossings of the annulus by loops, there exists a collection of conformal rectangles R m for 1 ≤ m ≤ 8 , with four distinguished marked boundary arcs satisfying conditions (i) and (ii) with the component U in place of U j and the arcs { I 1 , I 2 , I 3 , I 4 } in place of { I 1 j , I 2 j , I 3 j , I 4 j } .

Now let us give a brief outline of the proof of Lemma 4.7. Suppose that we have the same setup as in Lemma 4.7 and fix j ∈ { 1 , . . . , N } so that the boundary conditions of Ψ | U j are given by the zero function. We let ϕ j denote the unique conformal mapping from the simply connected domain U j onto the Euclidean rectangle V j def =(0 , L j ) × (0 , 1) for some L j ∈ (0 , ∞ ) such that ϕ j ( I 1 j ∩ ∂ out A ) = 0, ϕ j ( I 2 j ∩ ∂ out A ) = L j , ϕ j ( I 2 j ∩ ∂ in A ) = L j +i, and ϕ j ( I 1 j ∩ ∂ in A ) = i. We assume that the arc I 2 j lies to the right of I 1 j in the counterclockwise direction. We also assume that I 3 j (resp. I 4 j ) lies on the inner (resp. outer) boundary of A and that I 3 j ∩ ∂B 2 - 2 R/ 3 (0) = ∅ and I 4 j ∩ ∂B 2 - R/ 3 (0) = ∅ .

The first step will be to divide U j into ⌊ R ⌋ +1 conformal rectangles G j, 1 , . . . , G j, ⌊ R ⌋ +1 which satisfy property (i) in the statement of Lemma 4.7. Next, we will prove in Lemma 4.8 that the above conformal rectangles are not too wide in the sense that all of them have conformal modulus at most 1 /R provided R &gt; 1 is sufficiently large.

The next step will be to argue that with high probability, a positive fraction of the G j,n 's have the property that there is no loop in Γ intersecting both their top and bottom boundaries. This will be achieved in Lemma 4.9 using Lemma 4.8 by arguing that there exists a universal constant p ∈ (0 , 1) such that with conditional probability at least p , a level line of Ψ starting from some point on the left boundary of G j,n intersects the right boundary before intersecting either its top or bottom boundaries. This will prevent the loops in Γ from making a crossing of G j,n between its top and bottom boundaries due to the level line interaction rules (see [WW17, Theorem 1.1.4]).

Figure 6. Illustration of Lemma 4.7. On the left-hand side, we draw the connected component U j with its associated boundaries. The top boundary I 3 j is associated with the inner boundary of the annulus while the bottom boundary I 4 j is associated with the outer boundary of the annulus. The left and right boundaries I 1 j and I 2 j correspond to crossings by loops. The conformal rectangles R j, 1 , . . . , R j, 8 are drawn in blue. The top and bottom sides of the conformal rectangles U j, 1 , . . . , U j, 8 are drawn in green. On the right-hand side, one can see a conformal rectangle U j,m and its division into three sub-rectangles U 1 j,m , U 2 j,m and U 3 j,m satisfying Conditions (ii.a), (ii.b) and (ii.c).

Finally, we will show in Lemma 4.10 using Lemma 2.13 that the following holds with high probability. Among the conformal rectangles G j,n for which there is no loop in Γ making a crossing between their top and bottom boundaries, a positive fraction of them satisfies the following property. The D Γ -distances between their left and right boundaries are strictly smaller than the D Γ -distances between their top and bottom boundaries. We will deduce Lemma 4.7 by combining Lemmas 4.8, 4.9 and 4.10.

Remark 4.11 . Recall from Remark 4.6 that we can perform the exploration of the crossings of A made by loops in Γ with height in { (2 n - 1) λ, (2 n +1) λ } for fixed n ∈ Z in exactly the same way as in the case n = 0. Let U 1 , . . . , U N be the corresponding connected components obtained by these crossings (or excursions into A ). Note that for a component U j such that the boundary conditions of Ψ on ∂U j are equal to 2 λn , we have that the conditional law, given Ψ | D \ U j , of the collection of loops in Γ which are contained entirely in U j is the same as that of a nested CLE 4 on U j , and that (for n = 0) U j is necessarily surrounded by an outermost loop in Γ. Therefore, arguing exactly as in the proof of Lemma 4.7 (applied to the field Ψ | U j - 2 λn , which has zero boundary conditions on ∂U j and whose level loops of height in {- λ, λ } are precisely the loops of Γ that have height in { (2 n - 1) λ, (2 n +1) λ } ), we obtain the same statement for these components U j , with the same constants b, R 0 , and B ; in particular, these constants do not depend on n .

## 5. Good annuli

- 5.1. Outline and setup. In the present section, we are going to prove that with very high probability, there are many annuli which are good , i.e., which satisfy conditions (iii) and (iv) introduced in Section 3.2.

Throughout the present section, we let Ψ be a zero-boundary GFF on D and let Γ be a nested CLE 4 on D such that the pair (Ψ , Γ) is coupled as in Section 2.5, we let Γ denote the collection of outermost loops in Γ, and, as in Section 4, we fix R &gt; 1 and let A def = B 1 / 2 (0) \ B 2 - R (0).

First, we will show that an event E (Ψ), which encodes the information about A that we shall need in order to deduce that A is good , occurs with probability tending to 1 as R → ∞ . This is the content of the following lemma. Recall from Remark 4.5 the definition of U γ for γ ∈ P A in the case where no loop in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } crosses A . We let ϕ γ : U γ → V γ def = (0 , L γ ) × (0 , 1) be the unique conformal mapping from U γ that sends the left, right, top and bottom sides of U γ to the respective sides of V γ def = (0 , L γ ) × (0 , 1).

- Lemma 5.1. There exist universal constants α &gt; 2 and R 0 &gt; 1 such that for all R ≥ R 0 , there exists a constant c R &gt; 0 depending only on R such that the following is true. Suppose that we have the same setup as in Lemma 4.7 and let U 1 , . . . , U N denote the connected components of the complement in A of the union of the crossings of A made by loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } together with the interiors of the excursions into A from the inner and outer boundary of A made by such loops, as in Remark 4.6 with n = 0 . In the case that no loop in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } crosses A , we instead set N = 1 and let U 1 be as in Remark 4.5. Let E (Ψ) denote the event that the following hold.
- (i) There are at most four crossings of A made by loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } .
- (ii) For every j ∈ { 1 , . . . , N } such that the boundary conditions of Ψ | U j are given by the zero function, we have that (recall the definitions of ϕ j , V j and L j from Section 4.3)
