and in the case where there is no crossing of A by loops with height in {- λ, λ } , we have for all γ ∈ P A ,

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

Moreover, in the case that there are exactly four crossings of A made by loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } , there exists for each such j a collection of conformal rectangles R j,m ⊆ U j for 1 ≤ m ≤ 8 for which properties (i) and (ii) in the statement of Lemma 4.7 hold, with B the universal constant appearing there.

Then, we have that P [ E (Ψ)] ≥ 1 - 2 - αR .

Remark 5.2 . Note that Lemma 4.1 combined with the coupling between Ψ and Γ described in Section 2.5 together with Axiom II (locality) implies that the event E (Ψ) introduced in Lemma 5.1 is a.s. determined by Ψ | A together with the metrics D V j ϕ j (Γ | U j ) for 1 ≤ j ≤ N , which are conditionally independent given Ψ | A . Hence, if ˜ Ψ is a GFF on D whose boundary conditions are piecewise constant and change only a finite number of times, then since the laws of Ψ | A and ˜ Ψ | A are mutually absolutely continuous (see [MS16a, Proposition 3.4]), we can define the event E ( ˜ Ψ) in the exact same way that we defined E (Ψ) but with the field Ψ replaced by ˜ Ψ.

Moreover, for all z ∈ D and k ∈ N such that B 2 - k ( z ) ⊆ D , we can define the event E z,k (Ψ) in the same way that we defined E (Ψ) except that we explore the crossings of the annulus B 2 - k - 1 ( z ) \ B 2 - k - R ( z ) made by the loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } instead of the corresponding crossings of A . Note that the case z = 0, k = 0 corresponds to the annulus A used in the definition of E (Ψ). We emphasize that the notation E z,k (Ψ) suppresses the dependence on R , which will always be clear from the context. Finally, when the event is considered for a field whose boundary conditions are not identically equal to 0, the heights {- λ, λ } above are to be replaced by { (2 n - 1) λ, (2 n +1) λ } for the relevant n ∈ Z ; see Remarks 4.6 and 5.8.

Next, using Lemma 5.1 and Remark 5.2 combined with the independence across scales property of the GFF (see Section 3.2), we will show that there are many good annuli with sufficiently high probability. This is the main result of the present subsection and we state it in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.3. Let α &gt; 2 and R 0 &gt; 1 be the constants in Lemma 5.1. Suppose that we have the same setup as in Lemma 5.1 and Remark 5.2. Fix a compact set F ⊆ D and let ν ∈ (0 , 1) be such that α &gt; max { 2(1 + ν ) , 2 / (1 - ν ) 2 } ; such a ν exists since α &gt; 2 . Fix also R 1 ≥ R 0 . Then, it is a.s. the case that there exist R &gt; R 1 and K 0 ∈ N such that for all K ≥ K 0 and all z ∈ ( 1 100 2 - (1+ ν ) RK Z ) 2 ∩ F , there exists k ∈ [ νKR, (1 + ν ) KR - R ] Z such that E z,k (Ψ) occurs.

Let us now give a brief outline of the proof of Lemma 5.1. Condition (i) in the statement of Lemma 5.1 will follow from the following lemma, which states that the probability of having more than four crossings of A made by loops in Γ is sufficiently small. Its proof will be given in Section A.

Lemma 5.4. Let Γ be a nested CLE 4 on D , let F ⊆ D be a compact set, and set d def = dist( F, ∂ D ) &gt; 0 . Then, there exists a universal constant b 0 ∈ (0 , 1) such that the following is true. For each ϵ ∈ (0 , 1) , there exists δ ∈ (0 , 1) depending only on ϵ and d such that for all z ∈ F and all 0 &lt; r 1 &lt; r 2 &lt; d/ 2 with r 1 /r 2 ≤ δ , the probability that there are at least four crossings of B r 2 ( z ) \ B r 1 ( z ) made by loops in Γ is at most ( r 1 /r 2 ) 2 - ϵ . Also, the probability that there are more than four crossings of B r 2 ( z ) \ B r 1 ( z ) made by loops in Γ is at most ( r 1 /r 2 ) 2+ b 0 .

As for condition (ii) in the statement of Lemma 5.1, we would like to apply Lemma 4.7 to deduce the existence of conformal rectangles satisfying the desired properties. In order to do so, we have to verify the hypotheses of Lemma 4.7 that I 3 j ∩ ∂B 2 - 2 R/ 3 (0) = ∅ and I 4 j ∩ ∂B 2 - R/ 3 (0) = ∅ . Since I 3 j (resp. I 4 j ) is contained in the union of the excursions into A from the inner (resp. outer) boundary of A , this amounts to knowing that, with sufficiently high probability, the following holds. On the event that there are exactly four crossings of A made by loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } , there is no crossing of either B 1 / 2 (0) \ B 2 - R/ 3 (0) or B 2 - 2 R/ 3 (0) \ B 2 - R (0) made by a loop in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } other than the ones arising from the four crossings of A . This will be achieved in Lemma 5.7.

In the proof of Lemma 5.7, we are going to use the independence across scales property of the GFF (see Lemmas 5.5 and 5.6) to argue that there are many values of k ∈ [2 , R ] Z such that the following holds. Almost surely, the conditional law, given the values of Ψ on D \ B 2 - k (0), of Ψ - ⌊ Ψ 2 - k (0) / (2 λ ) ⌋ 2 λ restricted to B 2 - k - 1 (0) is mutually absolutely continuous with respect to the law of the restriction to B 2 - k - 1 (0) of a zero-boundary GFF on B 2 - k (0), and the corresponding Radon-Nikodym derivatives in both directions have conditional moments of all orders which are bounded by deterministic constants independent of k and R . Therefore, by combining these RadonNikodym derivative bounds with Lemma 5.4 applied to a nested CLE 4 on B 2 - k (0) coupled with a zero-boundary GFF on B 2 - k (0), and arguing in the same way for the crossings of B 2 - 2 R/ 3 (0) \ B 2 - R (0), we will obtain Lemma 5.7. Combining the latter with Lemmas 5.4 and 4.7 will then complete the proof of Lemma 5.1.

## 6. Covering geodesics by good annuli

In the present section, we will prove Proposition 3.4 and at the end of this section we will also obtain Proposition 3.5. As explained in Section 3.2, the main idea behind the proof of Proposition 3.4 is to cover any D Γ -geodesic P connecting any two loops in Γ by good annuli.

As a first step, we will use Lemma 5.3 to find annuli A 1 , . . . , A N - 1 satisfying the properties of the events E z,k (Ψ) introduced in Remark 5.2, in such a way that P is covered by the Euclidean balls bounded by the outer boundaries of the A j 's together with the loops in Γ which P intersects. However, Lemma 5.3 requires that the annuli be centered at points of a grid contained in a deterministic compact set F ⊆ D , and so we will need that P ⊆ F for some such F . Since the image of P is a compact subset of D , the latter follows from the fact that P does not intersect ∂ D , which was shown in Corollary 2.14.

Next, we would like to show that we can choose the annuli A 1 , . . . , A N - 1 so that there are exactly two loops in Γ crossing A j , for all 1 ≤ j ≤ N - 1 (see condition (iii) in Section 3.2). Note first that condition (i) in the definition of the events E z,k (Ψ) implies that there are at most two such loops, since a loop which crosses an annulus has to cross it at least twice. On the other hand, the definition of the events E z,k (Ψ) does not exclude the case where we have only one loop in Γ or no loops in Γ crossing the annulus. Nevertheless, condition (ii) in the definition of the E z,k (Ψ)'s implies that in both of these cases, the geodesic P has to travel D Γ -distance at least c R in order to cross the annulus, where c R &gt; 0 is the constant from Lemma 5.1. We will show in Lemma 6.1 (see also Lemma 6.2) that with very high probability, the latter does not happen. Thus, we will deduce that there are exactly two loops in Γ crossing A j and that each of them crosses A j exactly twice, for all 1 ≤ j ≤ N - 1.

Now, we note that the existence of the conformal rectangles introduced in Lemma 4.7 (which are indexed by the connected components of the complement in A j of the loops crossing A j , and not by the annuli themselves) implies that the following hold for all 1 ≤ j ≤ N - 1 (see Lemma 6.5). Whenever the geodesic P makes a crossing of A j , it has to intersect at least one of the two loops in Γ crossing A j . The same argument applies to any D Γ -geodesic connecting ∂ D with a loop in Γ which is separated from ∂ D by the two loops crossing A j together with the fifth of the conformal rectangles contained in each of the two components of A j cut out by these loops (the rectangles being numbered starting from the one closest to the outer boundary of A j ; the reason for taking the fifth one is explained in the proof of Proposition 3.4). This is the case in particular for every loop in Γ which P intersects while making a crossing of A j , which is the only situation in which we shall use it. Hence, combining with the fact that replacing an initial segment of a D Γ -geodesic by another D Γ -geodesic with the same endpoints again produces a D Γ -geodesic, we will deduce that (after possibly discarding some of the loops and of the annuli from the chain, so that P intersects both of the loops crossing A j and so that the second of these two loops is also the first of the two loops crossing A j +1 ) the part P j of P between the last time it intersects the first such loop and the first time it intersects the second satisfies the following property. Let t ∈ [0 , 1] be such that P j ( t ) ∈ ℓ for some ℓ ∈ Γ (no other case has to be considered in the definition of D 0 Γ , since P does not intersect ∂ D ). Then, there exists a D Γ -geodesic from ℓ to ∂ D containing (up to monotone reparameterization) either the time-reversal of P j | [0 ,t ] or P j | [ t, 1] , where we have parameterized P j by [0 , 1]; for t ∈ { 0 , 1 } the assertion is immediate, since any path contained in a single loop has zero D Γ -length. This will complete the proof of Proposition 3.4.

6.1. Some useful results. Recall that as explained at the beginning of the present section, we need that with very high probability, the following is true. Whenever the geodesic P and its timereversal both enter a small Euclidean ball before intersecting each other, the D Γ -length of the part of P which is left after removing the two corresponding initial segments has to be very small. In particular, whenever P makes a crossing of a small Euclidean annulus, the D Γ -length of that crossing has to be very small. This is the content of the following lemma.

̸

Lemma 6.1. Let F ⊆ D be a compact set and fix x, y ∈ F . Then, there exist a constant c 2 &gt; 0 (depending only on x, y , and F ) and, for each c &gt; 0 , constants R 0 = R 0 ( x, y, F, c ) &gt; 1 and c 1 = c 1 ( x, y, F, c ) &gt; 1 , such that the following is true for all c &gt; 0 and all R ≥ R 0 . Let ℒ (resp. ℒ ′ ) denote the loop in Γ surrounding x (resp. y ) and suppose that we are working on the event that ℒ = ℒ ′ . Let P be a D Γ -geodesic connecting ℒ to ℒ ′ viewed as a path from ℒ to ℒ ′ , and let ˜ P denote the time-reversal of P . Off an event with probability at most c 1 e - c 2 cR , we have that for all z ∈ F , if the paths P and ˜ P intersect B 2 - R ( z ) before intersecting each other, and if we let t (resp. ˜ t ) be the first time that P (resp. ˜ P ) intersects B 2 - R ( z ) , then we have that t ≤ 1 - ˜ t and that len( P | [ t, 1 - ˜ t ] ; D Γ ) , i.e. the D Γ -length of the part of P which is left after removing P | [0 ,t ] and the time-reversal of ˜ P | [0 , ˜ t ] , is strictly less than c .

The main ingredient of the proof of Lemma 6.1 is the following lemma.

Conditions (a)-(c) will play a crucial role in constructing chains of loops in Γ and good annuli which cover the geodesic P . Before proceeding to the explicit construction of the aforementioned chains of loops and annuli, we state and prove two useful lemmas. The first lemma (Lemma 6.4) states that the intersection of P with any loop in Γ is a segment of P . The second lemma (Lemma 6.5) states that whenever the geodesic P crosses an annulus for which the event E z,k (Ψ) occurs and there are two loops in Γ crossing the annulus, we have that P intersects at least one of the loops in Γ crossing the annulus.

̸

Lemma 6.4. Let ℓ be a loop in Γ such that P ∩ ℓ = ∅ . Then, there exist times 0 ≤ s ≤ t ≤ 1 such that P ∩ ℓ = P ([ s, t ]) .

̸

Proof. Let ℓ ∈ Γ be such that P ∩ ℓ = ∅ and let s (resp. t ) be the first (resp. last) time that P intersects ℓ . By the definitions of s and t , we have that P ∩ ℓ ⊆ P ([ s, t ]), and so it suffices to show that P ([ s, t ]) ⊆ ℓ .

We first claim that len( P | [ s,t ] ; D Γ ) = 0. Indeed, since P ( s ) , P ( t ) ∈ ℓ , we can replace P | [ s,t ] by an arc of ℓ from P ( s ) to P ( t ). The resulting path is admissible, it connects ℒ with ℒ ′ , and its D Γ -length is equal to len( P ; D Γ ) - len( P | [ s,t ] ; D Γ ) since any path which is contained in a single loop has zero D Γ -length. As P is a D Γ -geodesic, the claim follows.

Suppose now that ℓ ′ ∈ Γ is a loop which P | [ s,t ] intersects. Then the definition of len( · ; D Γ ) implies that

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

and hence ℓ ′ = ℓ since D Γ is a metric on Γ. Therefore every loop in Γ which P ([ s, t ]) intersects is equal to ℓ . Now, assume by contradiction that there is u ∈ ( s, t ) such that P ( u ) ̸∈ ℓ . By admissibility of P , we know that P ∩ ⋃ L ∈ Γ L is dense in P . Since ℓ is closed and P ( u ) ̸∈ ℓ , we deduce that there exists a sequence t n ∈ [0 , 1] and loops ℓ n ∈ Γ \ { ℓ } such that P ( t n ) ∈ ℓ n and P ( t n ) → P ( u ). By taking a subsequence, we may assume that t n → u ′ ∈ [0 , 1]. In particular, P ( u ′ ) = P ( u ). One can see that s &lt; u ′ &lt; t because otherwise, we can remove from P the piece of path P ([ u ′ , u ]) or P ([ u, u ′ ]) and obtain an admissible path with shorter D Γ -length. But then, for n large enough, we have t n ∈ ( s, t ), which is absurd since we have shown that every loop in Γ which intersects P ([ s, t ]) is equal to ℓ . Therefore, P ([ s, t ]) ⊂ ℓ . This completes the proof of the lemma. □

Lemma 6.5. Suppose that we have the same setup as in Corollary 6.3, so that in particular R and P are as fixed above. Let z ∈ D , k ∈ N be such that B 2 - k ( z ) ⊆ D and E z,k (Ψ) occurs. Suppose also that the collection of loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } which cross B 2 - k - 1 ( z ) \ B 2 - k - R ( z )

is non-empty and that there are at least two loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } which cross B 2 - k - 1 ( z ) \ B 2 - k - R ( z ) . Let ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 denote the elements of the above collection (recall that, since E z,k (Ψ) occurs, it consists of at most two loops; we use the letters ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 rather than ℒ 1 , ℒ 2 in order to avoid a clash with the chain of loops constructed in Section 6.2). Then, wherever the geodesic P makes a crossing of B 2 - k - 1 ( z ) \ B 2 - k - R ( z ) (in particular, whenever it makes a crossing of the larger annulus B 2 - k ( z ) \ B 2 - k - R ( z ) ), the portion of P which makes that crossing must intersect either ℓ 1 or ℓ 2 . More generally, the same conclusion holds whenever P crosses one of the connected components U j of the complement in B 2 - k - 1 ( z ) \ B 2 - k - R ( z ) of the crossings of that annulus made by loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } together with the interiors of the excursions into it made by such loops (we use the notation of Lemma 5.1), between the two marked boundary arcs I 3 j and I 4 j of ∂U j .

Proof. Step 1. Setup. Suppose that P makes a crossing of B 2 - k ( z ) \ B 2 - k - R ( z ). We will assume that it crosses the annulus from its outer to its inner boundary since the exact same argument works whenever it makes a crossing from its inner to its outer boundary.

Step 2. The crossing loops ℓ 1 and ℓ 2 . Let 0 ≤ s &lt; t ≤ 1 be such that P ( s ) ∈ ∂B 2 - k ( z ) , P ( t ) ∈ ∂B 2 - k - R ( z ) , P (( s, t )) ⊆ B 2 - k ( z ) \ B 2 - k - R ( z ), and P ([ s, t ]) is part of the crossing that P makes. Note that P | [ s,t ] contains in particular a crossing of the smaller annulus B 2 - k - 1 ( z ) \ B 2 - k - R ( z ) from its outer to its inner boundary. Note also that the definition of E z,k (Ψ) implies that there are at most four crossings of B 2 - k - 1 ( z ) \ B 2 - k - R ( z ) made by loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } , and hence at most two such loops, since a loop which crosses an annulus has to cross it at least twice. Consequently, ℓ 1 and ℓ 2 are the only loops in Γ with height in {- λ, λ } which cross B 2 - k - 1 ( z ) \ B 2 - k - R ( z ).

Step 3. The case where P | [ s,t ] avoids ℓ 1 and ℓ 2 . Suppose that P | [ s,t ] does not intersect either ℓ 1 or ℓ 2 . Then, using the notation of Lemma 5.1, we obtain that there exists j ∈ { 1 , . . . , N } such that the field Ψ | U j has zero-boundary conditions and P | [ s,t ] makes a crossing of U j from I 4 j to I 3 j . Indeed, P | [ s,t ] has to cross some component U j from I 4 j to I 3 j since it does not intersect ℓ 1 or ℓ 2 ; moreover, if the boundary conditions of Ψ | U j were not equal to zero, then Lemma 4.4 would imply that U j ⊆ int( ℓ 0 ) for some outermost loop ℓ 0 in Γ, i.e. for some ℓ 0 ∈ Γ; this is impossible, since int( ℓ 0 ) is an open set which does not intersect any loop in Γ (the loops in Γ being pairwise non-nested), so that the admissibility of P forces P ∩ int( ℓ 0 ) = ∅ . Then, condition (i) in Lemma 4.7 (and, more precisely, the fact that the left and right boundaries of R j,m are contained in I 1 j and I 2 j , so that R j,m separates I 3 j from I 4 j in U j ) implies that P | [ s,t ] has to cross all of the topological rectangles R j,m for 1 ≤ m ≤ 4 from the bottom to their top boundaries. In particular, the path P | [ s,t ] has to intersect all of the paths P j,m for 1 ≤ m ≤ 4, where P j,m denotes an admissible path in U j,m connecting the left and right marked boundary arcs of R j,m with len( P j,m ; D Γ ) &lt; B , the existence of which is guaranteed by condition (ii) in Lemma 4.7 and by the definition of the internal metric D U j,m Γ | U j,m . Let τ j,m denote the first time after s that P intersects P j,m . Then, the path P | [ τ j, 1 ,τ j, 4 ] must cross from bottom to top both R j, 2 and R j, 3 , and hence condition (ii) in Lemma 4.7 implies that the D Γ -length of P | [ τ j, 1 ,τ j, 4 ] is at least 2 B (here we use that R j, 2 and R j, 3 are disjoint and that the D Γ -length of a path contained in U j,m is at least the D U j,m Γ | U j,m -distance between its endpoints). However, concatenating the paths P j, 1 and P j, 4 with the loops ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 (more precisely, we follow P j, 1 from P ( τ j, 1 ) until we reach I 1 j , we then travel along the loop containing I 1 j until we reach the endpoint of P j, 4 on I 1 j , which costs no D Γ -length, and we finally follow P j, 4 until P ( τ j, 4 )) and noting that the D Γ -lengths of P j, 1 and P j, 4 are strictly less than B (see condition (ii) in Lemma 4.7), we obtain an admissible path from P ( τ j, 1 ) to P ( τ j, 4 ) whose D Γ -length is strictly less than 2 B . This implies that we can construct an admissible path connecting ℒ to ℒ ′ whose D Γ -length is strictly less than that of P . But this contradicts the fact that P is a D Γ -geodesic. It follows that P | [ s,t ] must intersect either ℓ 1 or ℓ 2 . Finally, we note that the argument of the previous paragraph used nothing about P | [ s,t ] beyond the fact that it crosses the component U j between I 3 j and I 4 j , and that the component crossed by P automatically has zero boundary conditions, by the argument given there. This proves the second assertion of the lemma as well. This completes the proof of the lemma. □

## 7. Measurability

By Theorem 3.1, we know that the metric of a geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling in the sense of Definition 1.2 is a measurable function of the associated uniform exploration; in particular, given a uniform exploration of the CLE 4 , there is at most one such metric whose metric balls from the boundary agree with this uniform exploration. The following result shows that the metric, and in particular the uniform exploration, is a.s. determined by the CLE 4 , thus implying that the metric of any geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling coincides a.s. with a strong CLE 4 metric in the sense of Definition 1.3. This will complete the proofs of Theorems 1.7 and 1.5 and will enable us to prove Corollary 1.6.

Proposition 7.1. Let D be a geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling. Let Γ U be a non-nested CLE 4 in a deterministic simply connected domain U ⊊ C . Then D U Γ U is a.s. determined by Γ U ; that is, D U Γ U is a.s. equal to a measurable function of Γ U .

The above proposition allows us to upgrade every geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling to a strong CLE 4 metric, as follows.

Proposition 7.2. Let D be a geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling. Then there exists a strong CLE 4 metric ˜ D in the sense of Definition 1.3 such that, for every simply connected domain U ⊊ C , we have D U Γ U = ˜ D U Γ U a.s.

Proof of Proposition 7.2 assuming Proposition 7.1. By Proposition 7.1 and the Doob-Dynkin lemma, for each simply connected domain U ⊊ C there is a measurable mapping ˜ D U : ℒℴℴ𝓅ℰ𝓃𝓈 ( U ) → R U Q × U Q such that D U Γ U = ˜ D U Γ U a.s. It remains to check that the family

˜ D = { ˜ D U } U satisfies the axioms of Definition 1.3. Axioms (I) (geodesic metric) and (IV) (uniform exploration) are immediate from Axioms (I) and (IV) of Definition 1.2, since ˜ D U Γ U = D U Γ U a.s. and these two axioms only involve the law of the pair (Γ U , D U Γ U ). Next, we check Axiom (II) (locality). Let V ⊆ U be a deterministic simply connected subdomain and let { V j } j be the connected components of V ⋆ . By Axiom (II) of Definition 1.2, conditionally on (1.2), the pair (Γ U | V j , D U Γ U ( · , · ; V j )) has the law of (Γ V j , D V j Γ V j ), and hence also the law of (Γ V j , ˜ D V j Γ V j ). Since in the latter pair the second coordinate is a.s. equal to a fixed measurable function of the first, the same must be true of the former, i.e. a.s. D U Γ U ( · , · ; V j ) = ˜ D V j Γ U | V j for every j . Finally, we check Axiom (III) (conformal invariance). Let ϕ : U → ϕ ( U ) be a deterministic conformal mapping. By Axiom (III) of Definition 1.2, the pairs

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

have the same law. In both pairs the second coordinate is a measurable function of the first, and the first coordinates have the same law (by the conformal invariance of the CLE 4 ), so the two measurable functions agree almost everywhere with respect to the law of a non-nested CLE 4 in ϕ ( U ). This is precisely Axiom (III). □

Proof of Theorem 1.5 assuming Proposition 7.1. Assertion (i) is exactly Proposition 7.2. As for assertion (ii), let D and ˜ D be two strong CLE 4 metrics and let U ⊊ C be a deterministic simply connected domain. Each of D and ˜ D gives rise to a geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling, so that, by Theorem 3.1, each of D U Γ U and ˜ D U Γ U is given by one and the same measurable function of the associated uniform exploration; since Γ U is itself a measurable function of that exploration, and since the law of the latter does not depend on the metric by Axiom (IV) (uniform exploration), the couplings (Γ U , D U Γ U ) and (Γ U , ˜ D U Γ U ) have the same law. Since D U Γ U and ˜ D U Γ U are both measurable functions of Γ U , it follows that D U and ˜ D U agree almost everywhere with respect to the law of a non-nested CLE 4 in U , i.e. D U Γ U = ˜ D U Γ U a.s. □

Proof of Theorem 1.7 assuming Proposition 7.1. By [KKMT26a, Theorem 1.3], the family of the laws of the metrics a - 1 κ D U Γ κ U for κ ∈ (4 , 8) is tight with respect to the topology of R U Q × U Q , and every subsequential limit as κ ↓ 4 is the metric of a weak geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling. By [KKMT26b, Theorem 1.4], every weak geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling is a geodesic CLE 4 metric coupling, and by Theorem 3.1 the law of the latter is unique; by Proposition 7.2, it is the law of (Γ U , D U Γ U ) for the strong CLE 4 metric D of Theorem 1.5. Since all subsequential limits agree in law, the convergence in law follows. □

The proof of Proposition 7.1 will follow the same idea as in [MS20, Section 7], which constructed a metric on √ 8 / 3-Liouville quantum gravity that was later shown in [MS21a, MS21b] to be isometric to the Brownian sphere. However, since in our case the metric has already been constructed, the proof will be much easier.

We therefore conclude that the BCLE ⟳ 4 ( ρ ) is a.s. determined by Γ D . □

## 8. Uniqueness of geodesics

In the present section, we prove that there is a.s. a unique geodesic connecting any pair of loops. More precisely, we prove Theorem 1.8. Let D be the (strong) CLE 4 metric (which exists and is unique by Theorem 1.5). By conformal invariance (Axiom (III) of Definition 1.3), together with the fact that a conformal transformation maps admissible paths to admissible paths and hence D -geodesics to D -geodesics, it is enough to prove the following reformulation of Theorem 1.8.

Theorem 8.1 (Uniqueness of geodesics) . Almost surely, for each ℒ 1 , ℒ 2 ∈ Γ H , there is a unique D H Γ H -geodesic connecting ℒ 1 and ℒ 2 , in the sense that if P 1 and P 2 are D H Γ H -geodesics connecting ℒ 1 and ℒ 2 , then { ℒ ∈ Γ H : ℒ ∩ P 1 = ∅} = { ℒ ∈ Γ H : ℒ ∩ P 2 = ∅} . Suppose that F ( d ) ≥ F ( d ′ ) and G ( d ) ≥ G ( d ′ ) for all d, d ′ ∈ R U Q × U Q with d ( x, y ) ≥ d ′ ( x, y ) for all x, y ∈ U Q . Then E [ F ( D U Γ U ) G ( D U Γ U )] ≥ E [ F ( D U Γ U )] E [ G ( D U Γ U )] . The same is true for any pair of non-negative measurable functionals of (Γ U , D U Γ U ) which are a.s. non-increasing under the merging operation of Lemma 8.2, and in particular for the indicator functions of the events { D U Γ U ( A,B ) &gt; ε } , where A,B ⊆ U are deterministic and ε &gt; 0 .

Proof. This follows immediately from Lemmas 8.2 and 8.3 combined with the fact that D U Γ U is a.s. determined by the critical Brownian loop soup used to construct Γ U since D U Γ U is a.s. determined by Γ U (Proposition 7.1). □

Next, let us state the primary ingredient in the proof of Theorem 8.1. Its own proof will be given at the end of the present section.

Proposition 8.5. Let r &gt; 0 . Let V r = (0 , r ) × (0 , 1) . Write T r (resp. B r ) for the top (resp. bottom) side of V r . Then the law of the random variable D V r Γ Vr ( T r , B r ) is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

The proof of Theorem 8.1 is reminiscent of the proof of geodesic uniqueness for LQG metrics (cf. [MQ20, DDG23]). The idea is as follows. Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that there are two distinct geodesics connecting a given pair of loops. Then, there exists a dyadic domain such that one of the geodesics passes through it while the other does not. Moreover, the former geodesic enters and exits this dyadic domain through two specific loops. There are only countably many choices for such a dyadic domain and its corresponding entrance and exit loops. However, if we condition on everything not fully contained within the dyadic domain, the length of the latter geodesic becomes deterministic. In contrast, the length of the former geodesic depends on the internal distance between the entrance and exit loops inside the dyadic domain, which, by Proposition 8.5, is random. Thus, the conditional probability that the two geodesics have exactly the same length is zero, yielding a contradiction.

Proof of Theorem 8.1 assuming Proposition 8.5. Step 1. Dyadic domains, excursion arcs, and the σ -algebra 𝒢 W . We shall refer to as a dyadic square a square with side length 2 - n and vertices in (2 - n Z ) 2 for some n ∈ Z . We shall refer to a simply connected domain W ⊆ C as dyadic if there exists a finite collection of dyadic squares such that W is the interior of the union of their closures. Note that the set of dyadic domains is countable. (Simple connectivity is imposed so that the connected components of W ⋆ are simply connected, so that Γ O and D O are defined for such a component O .)

Let us first fix some notation. Let W be a dyadic domain with W ⊆ H and z 1 , z 2 / ∈ W , and let O be a connected component of W ⋆ , so that O is simply connected. If ℒ ∈ Γ H is a loop which is not contained in W , then each connected component of ℒ ∩ W is an open sub-arc of ℒ , and we call the closure of such a component an excursion arc provided it is contained in ∂O . There are countably many excursion arcs, and, together with W ⋆ and with the collection of its connected components, they are determined by Γ H \ Γ H | W ⋆ . Finally, let 𝒢 W denote the σ -algebra generated by (1.2) with V = W , i.e. by the loops of Γ H which are not contained in W together with the metric balls started from those loops and from ∂ H and stopped upon hitting ∂W ⋆ . It remains to prove Proposition 8.5. The proof strategy proceeds in stages. First, we state a lemma (Lemma 8.6) which gives that the distance between the top and bottom sides of the rectangle V r can be arbitrarily large with positive probability. This lemma will be a consequence of the FKG inequality (cf. Lemma 8.4). Next, we establish a weaker version of Proposition 8.5 in Lemma 8.7. Using the notation of Proposition 8.5, this preliminary lemma states that if r is a random variable which is independent of the CLE 4 and whose law is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, then the distance D V r Γ Vr ( T r , B r ) satisfies the desired property.

The proof of Lemma 8.7 proceeds as follows. We recall that in a non-nested CLE 4 on the unit disk, the distance from the origin-containing loop to the domain boundary is an exponentially distributed random variable (this is immediate from Axiom (IV) together with the conformal invariance of the metric, which makes the exploration seen from the origin memoryless). Consequently, removing the origin-containing loop from the unit disk yields a random annulus whose cross-distance is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. We then transition from this random annulus to a random rectangle by conditioning on all loops that intersect a fixed line segment crossing the annulus.

Finally, we deduce Proposition 8.5 from Lemma 8.7 via a metric ball exploration. We initially run the metric ball exploration from the bottom boundary for a short duration. Applying Lemma 8.8, we can deduce that the unexplored region forms a random rectangle whose conformal modulus is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. This observation immediately reduces the proof of Proposition 8.5 to that of Lemma 8.7, the point being that, by the Markov property of the exploration, the conformal modulus of the unexplored region is independent of the CLE 4 inside it, so that Lemma 8.7 may be applied conditionally on that modulus.

Let us now turn to the first of these steps. The following result states that the distance across a rectangle can be arbitrarily large with positive probability. This is a direct consequence of the FKG inequality (cf. Lemma 8.4).

In order to prove the latter fact, we will use Lemmas 8.8 and 8.9 below, which prove the analogous property for the regions in the complement of an SLE 4 curve.

Lemma 8.8. Let x, y ∈ ( -∞ , 0) and z ∈ (0 , 1) be random points. Suppose that, conditionally on z , the law of the pair ( x, y ) is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on ( -∞ , 0) 2 . Given x , y , and z , let η be a chordal SLE 4 curve in H from x to y . Then the law of the conformal modulus of the conformal rectangle ( H \ η ; 0 , z, 1 , ∞ ) is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

Throughout this subsection, whenever η is a curve in H with both endpoints on ∂ H ∩ ( -∞ , 0) and K ⊆ H is a compact set, we write ( H \ ( η ∪ K ); 0 , z, 1 , ∞ ) for the conformal rectangle whose underlying domain is the unbounded connected component of H \ ( η ∪ K ).

Lemma 8.9. Suppose that we have the exact same setup as in Lemma 8.8. Then, conditionally on z , if L 0 denotes the conformal modulus of ( H ; 0 , z, 1 , ∞ ) , we have that the restriction of the Lebesgue measure to [0 , L 0 ] is absolutely continuous with respect to the conditional law of the conformal modulus of ( H \ η ; 0 , z, 1 , ∞ ) .

Note that L 0 is a function of z alone, so that the statement makes sense conditionally on z .

Before giving the proofs of Lemmas 8.8 and 8.9, we state and prove two simple but useful lemmas.

Lemma 8.10. Let K ⊂ H be a compact subset with finitely many connected components, each of which meets ∂ H , such that H \ K has a unique unbounded connected component H K , and let u = v be two points of ∂ H \ K lying on ∂H K . Fix a conformal mapping ϕ from H K onto H . Let η be a chordal SLE 4 curve in H from u to v . Then, the law of ϕ ( η ) conditionally on the event that η ∩ K = ∅ is mutually absolutely continuous with respect to the law of a chordal SLE 4 curve in H from ϕ ( u ) to ϕ ( v ) .

Proof. This follows from [Law09, Theorem 4.55]. (The case in which K is disconnected follows from the connected case by iterating.) □

Lemma 8.11. Let f : [0 , 1] → R be a continuous and strictly increasing function. Then f ′ ( x ) exists for almost every x ∈ [0 , 1] and

Proof. Write µ f for the measure on [0 , 1] associated with f (i.e., µ f ([ a, b ]) = f ( b ) - f ( a )). Since f is monotone, f ′ exists almost everywhere by Lebesgue's differentiation theorem for monotone functions. By the Lebesgue decomposition theorem, we have µ f = µ ac + µ s , where µ ac is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure and µ s is singular. Then Leb( f ( A )) = µ f ( A ) ≥ µ ac ( A ) = ∫ A f ′ ( x ) d x , where the last equality comes from the fact that µ ac = f ′ ( x )d x (see e.g. [Rud87, Theorem 7.14]). For the first equality, note that since f is continuous and strictly increasing it is a homeomorphism onto its image, so that f ( A ) is a Borel set, and that the Borel measures A ↦→ Leb( f ( A )) and µ f agree on intervals and hence coincide. □

By composing with an affine map and exhausting by compact subintervals, the same conclusion holds with [0 , 1] replaced by an arbitrary interval I ⊆ R and Borel A ⊆ I . ## Appendix A. Nested CLE 4 four-arm exponents

A.1. Statement of the main result and outline of the proof strategy. The main result that we are going to prove in this appendix is the following estimate on four-arm events for nested CLE 4 . Throughout this appendix we set C ε def = B 1 / 2 (0) \ B ε (0). This is the first main input in the proof of Lemma 5.4.

Proposition A.1. Fix ν ∈ (0 , 1) . Then, there exists ε 0 ∈ (0 , 1) depending only on ν such that the following holds for all ε ∈ (0 , ε 0 ) . Off an event with probability at most ε 2(1 - ν ) , we have that there are at most two crossings of the annulus C ε made by loops in Γ ; in particular, at most one loop of Γ crosses C ε .

We are also going to prove the following estimate for the probability of the event that there are at least six crossings of C ε made by loops in Γ; recall that the number of such crossings is necessarily even. This is the second main input in the proof of Lemma 5.4.

Proposition A.2. There exist universal constants b 0 , ε 0 ∈ (0 , 1) such that the following is true for all ε ∈ (0 , ε 0 ) . Off an event with probability at most ε 2+ b 0 , we have that there are at most four crossings of C ε made by loops in Γ ; in particular, at most two loops of Γ cross C ε .

Remark A.3 . Propositions A.1 and A.2 hold, with the same proofs, with C ε replaced by B r (0) \ B ε (0) for any fixed r ∈ (0 , 1 / 2], with ε 0 then depending also on r . Indeed, the only role of the outer radius 1 / 2 is to accommodate the annuli A k = B 2 - 3 k - 1 (0) \ B 2 - 3 k - 2 (0), k ≥ 1, used below, and these already lie inside B 1 / 16 (0); for smaller r one simply lets k range over the integers in [ k r , a log(1 /ε )], where k r is the least integer with 2 - 3 k r - 1 ≤ r and where a ∈ (0 , 1 / 100) is a small constant appearing in the proofs of Propositions A.1 and A.2.

Proof of Lemma 5.4. Step 1. The case r 2 ≥ d/ 8 . Let ϕ be the Möbius transformation of D with ϕ ( z ) = 0 and ϕ ′ ( z ) &gt; 0, and recall that the law of Γ is invariant under ϕ . Since r 2 &lt; d/ 2 ≤ (1 - | z | ) / 2, the Koebe distortion theorem gives universal constants c 1 , c 2 ∈ (0 , ∞ ) such that B c 1 r 2 / (1 -| z | 2 ) (0) ⊆ ϕ ( B r 2 ( z )) and ϕ ( B r 1 ( z )) ⊆ B c 2 r 1 / (1 -| z | 2 ) (0). More precisely, by [Law05, Corollary 3.25], one can take c 1 = 1 / 16 and c 2 = 16. As r 2 ≥ d/ 8 and 1 -| z | 2 ∈ [ d, 2], the radius r def = c 1 r 2 / (1 -| z | 2 ) is bounded below by c 1 d/ 16, which only depends on d ; moreover, r ≤ 1 / 2 since c 1 ≤ 1 and r 2 &lt; d/ 2 ≤ (1 -| z | ) / 2 ≤ (1 -| z | 2 ) / 2. Also c 2 r 1 / (1 -| z | 2 ) = ( c 2 /c 1 ) r · ( r 1 /r 2 ). Every crossing of B r 2 ( z ) \ B r 1 ( z ) therefore yields, under ϕ , a crossing of the concentric annulus with outer radius r and inner radius ( c 2 /c 1 ) r ( r 1 /r 2 ), and hence also a crossing of the annulus with the fi xed outer radius r ∗ def = min { c 1 d/ 16 , 1 / 2 } ≤ r and the same inner radius. Both claims now follow from Remark A.3, applied to Propositions A.1 and A.2 with the radius r ∗ , upon taking ν there smaller than ϵ/ 2 and δ small enough to absorb the constants.

Step 2. The case r 2 &lt; d/ 8 . Here conformal invariance alone does not suffice, since the image ϕ ( B r 2 ( z )) is then much smaller than D , while the bound we are proving is in terms of the ratio r 1 /r 2 alone. We therefore insert a comparison of scales. Write b 0 for the universal constant in Proposition A.2, set ϵ 1 def = min { ϵ, b 0 } / 8, and let K 1 (resp. K 2 ) be the smallest (resp. largest) integer k with 2 - k ≤ 2 r 2 (resp. 2 - k ≥ r 2 ( r 1 /r 2 ) ϵ 1 ), so that K 1 &gt; k 0 with k 0 = ⌊ log(1 / (2 r 2 )) / log 2 ⌋ , and K 2 - K 1 ≥ ϵ 1 2 log 2 log( r 2 /r 1 ) provided r 1 /r 2 is small enough. We use Lemma 5.5 in the following form, which follows from the same proof: given α ∈ (0 , ∞ ) and β 0 ∈ (0 , 1), there are constants c 0 , M &gt; 0 depending only on F, α and β 0 such that, for all L 2 &gt; L 1 ≥ k 0 , off an event of probability at most c 0 e - α ( L 2 - L 1 ) there are at least β 0 ( L 2 - L 1 ) values of k ∈ [ L 1 , L 2 ] Z for which E M z,k occurs. (Only the length of the window matters, since P [ E M z,k | F z,k - 1 ] is bounded from below uniformly in k and z .) Taking α large enough in a way depending only on ϵ and b 0 , we obtain that off an event of probability at most 1 2 ( r 1 /r 2 ) 3 there exists k ∈ [ K 1 , K 2 ] Z for which E M z,k occurs.

Fix such a k . Since 2 - k - 1 ≤ r 2 , every crossing of B r 2 ( z ) \ B r 1 ( z ) contains a crossing of B 2 - k - 1 ( z ) \ B r 1 ( z ), so it suffices to bound the probability of four (resp. more than four) crossings of the latter annulus. As B 2 - k - 1 ( z ) ⊆ B 15 · 2 - k / 16 ( z ), Lemma 5.6 together with Hölder's inequality transfers that event to a nested CLE 4 on B 2 - k ( z ) coupled with a zero-boundary GFF there, at the cost of raising its probability to a power 1 /q with q &gt; 1 as close to 1 as we like and multiplying by a constant depending only on q and M . Rescaling B 2 - k ( z ) to D turns B 2 - k - 1 ( z ) \ B r 1 ( z ) into precisely the annulus C 2 k r 1 , and 2 k r 1 ≤ ( r 1 /r 2 ) 1 - ϵ 1 by the choice of K 2 . Hence Propositions A.1 and A.2 bound the probability in question by ( r 1 /r 2 ) 2(1 - ν )(1 - ϵ 1 ) /q and by ( r 1 /r 2 ) (2+ b 0 )(1 - ϵ 1 ) /q , respectively. Taking ν def = ϵ 1 and then q close enough to 1, the first exponent is at least 2 - ϵ and the second is at least 2 + b 0 / 2; decreasing δ so as to absorb the multiplicative constants, we obtain the two assertions of the lemma, with the universal constant there given by b 0 / 2. □

The first main step of the proof of Proposition A.1 is to reduce the claim of the proposition to the case where we consider crossings of C ε made by finitely many level lines of a Gaussian free field, where the number of these level lines is bounded by a large but fixed and deterministic N ∈ N . This will be done in Section A.2.

In order to achieve this, we fix a ∈ (0 , 1) small and consider the coupling (Ψ , Γ) introduced in Section 2.5, where Ψ is a zero-boundary GFF on D . More precisely, for all k ∈ N 0 , we consider the annuli A k = B 2 - 3 k - 1 (0) \ B 2 - 3 k - 2 (0) and inside every such annulus, we consider the exploration of crossings of the annulus made by level lines of Ψ, as in Section 4.2. Then, since the exploration discovers finitely many crossings a.s. and the crossings are a.s. determined by the restrictions of Ψ to the corresponding annuli, we have by the independence across scales property of the GFF (see [GM20, Lemma 3.1]) that there exists N ∈ N sufficiently large such that the following holds. Off an event with probability at most ε 3 , for all sufficiently small ε , there exists k ∈ [1 , a log(1 /ε )] Z such that at most N of the level line crossings discovered cross A k between its inner and outer boundaries. We let k f be the smallest k ∈ [1 , a log(1 /ε )] Z for which the above occurs.

Suppose that there exists a loop ℓ ∈ Γ which crosses C ε . Then, every time that ℓ crosses C ε , it must first cross A k f . By using the level line interaction rules and arguing as in the proof of Lemma 4.1, we obtain that every time the loop ℓ crosses A k f , it has to merge with one of the level line crossings of A k f . In particular, if there are at least four crossings of C ε made by loops in Γ, then we can find four distinct level line crossings η 1 , η 2 , η 3 , η 4 of A k f such that their continuations after they exit A k f for the first time cross the annulus B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0) \ B ε (0), and hence, since k f ≤ a log(1 /ε ), also the annulus B ε 3 a log(2) / 4 (0) \ B ε (0). Therefore, it suffices to prove that the conditional probability of the event that, for every j ∈ { 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } , the path η j crosses B ε 3 a log(2) / 4 (0) \ B ε (0) before intersecting any of the other three paths, is at most ε 2(1 - ν ) for all sufficiently small ε . This will complete the proof of Proposition A.1. We will prove this in Section A.3 and then conclude the proof in Section A.4.

The main ingredient of the proof of the latter claim is the following proposition. Its proof will be given in Section A.4.3.

Proposition A.4. Let z 1 , z 2 , z 3 , z 4 be points on ∂ D ordered in the counterclockwise way, fix σ 1 , σ 3 ∈ {- 1 , +1 } , and let h be a GFF on D whose boundary conditions are given by 2 σ 1 λ on the counterclockwise arc of ∂ D from z 1 to z 2 , by 2 σ 3 λ on the counterclockwise arc of ∂ D from z 3 to z 4 , and by 0 on the two remaining arcs. For j ∈ { 1 , 3 } , let P j be the level line of σ j h with height λ from z j targeted at z j +1 , and let P j +1 be the level line of - σ j h with height - λ from z j +1 targeted at z j .

Then, there exists a universal constant C ∈ (0 , ∞ ) such that the following holds for all ε ∈ (0 , 1) . The probability of the event that none of the paths P 1 , P 2 , P 3 , P 4 reaches its continuation threshold and that each of them intersects ∂B ε (0) is at most Cε 2 .

Proposition A.4 will follow from Lemma 2.7, and its proof is given at the end of the appendix.

Let us now briefly explain how to conclude the proof of Proposition A.1 assuming Proposition A.4. Fix ρ ∈ (0 , a ) and ζ ∈ (0 , ρ/ 2), and cover the annulus B ε 3 a log(2) / 4 (0) \ B ε (0) by n ε ≍ ( ρ/ 2 + ζ ) - 1 successive annuli, each with a ratio of radii of order ε ρ/ 2+ ζ . Proposition A.4 implies that, conditionally on η 1 , η 2 , η 3 , η 4 stopped upon reaching the outer boundary of one of these annuli, the conditional probability that all four of them reach its inner boundary before intersecting one another is O ( ε ρ ). Multiplying these conditional bounds over the n ε annuli, we obtain that the probability that all four paths reach ∂B ε (0) before intersecting one another is O ( ε ρn ε ); since ρn ε → 2 as ζ/ρ → 0 and then a → 0, this completes the proof of the claim.

Finally, we give a brief overview of the proof of Proposition A.2. It will be a consequence of Proposition A.1 together with the method introduced in Section 4.3. More precisely, we fix ν ∈ (0 , 1) and ε ∈ (0 , 1), and suppose that we are working on the event that there are at least four crossings of C ε made by loops in Γ; we let ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 , ℓ 3 , ℓ 4 be four of them, labeled in counterclockwise order, and we set ℓ 5 def = ℓ 1 . For all j ∈ { 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } , we let G j denote the connected component of C ε \ ⋃ 4 i =1 ℓ i whose boundary contains both the right side of ℓ j and the left side of ℓ j +1 . Then, arguing as in Section 4.3, we will show that off an event with conditional probability at most ε b 0 as ε → 0 (for some universal constant b 0 &gt; 0), we have that the following holds. For all j ∈ { 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } , we can divide G j into disjoint conformal rectangles G j, 1 , . . . , G j,n as in Section 4.3 for n ≍ log(1 /ε ), such that for some n j ∈ { 1 , . . . , n } , there exists a level line P j of Ψ which makes a crossing in G j,n j from left to right. The level lines P 1 , P 2 , P 3 , P 4 will prevent any other loop in Γ from making a crossing of C ε due to the level line interaction rules.

Combining with Proposition A.1, we obtain that off an event with probability at most ε 2(1 - ν )+ b 0 as ε → 0, we have that there exist at most four crossings of C ε made by loops in Γ. Therefore, Proposition A.2 follows upon taking ν def = b 0 / 4 and replacing b 0 by b 0 / 2, so that 2(1 - ν ) + b 0 = 2 + b 0 / 2.

A.2. Reducing the claim to finitely many crossings. In this section, we will show that for fixed a ∈ (0 , 1), with very high probability as ε → 0, there is some k ∈ [1 , a log(1 /ε )] Z for which A k is crossed by at most N level lines of Ψ. As explained in Section A.1, every time that a loop in Γ makes a crossing of C ε , it has to merge with one of the aforementioned crossings. This reduces the claim of Proposition A.1 to proving estimates on the probability of four-arm events for finitely many level lines of Ψ.

We will use the same exploration as in Section 4.2. More precisely, we consider the rectangles R D , R U , and R I as in Section 4.2, and perform the level line explorations of the field Ψ. Note that we have already shown in Lemma 4.2 that both of the explorations in R D and R U terminate in finitely many steps a.s. Recall from Section 4.2 that each of the crossings discovered in R D and R U will continue into R I and either form a crossing of R I or make an excursion into R I back to R q for q ∈ { D,U } .

We have the following analog of Lemma 4.1.

Lemma A.5. The collection of segments of loops in Γ which are contained in A 0 and whose endpoints lie on ∂A 0 is a.s. determined by the restriction of the field Ψ to A 0 .

Proof. The claim in the statement of the lemma follows from the exact same argument used to prove Lemma 4.1. □

We can perform the same exploration described above but with the annulus A k in place of A 0 for all k ∈ N 0 . Then, the argument used to prove Lemma A.5 implies that the segments of loops in Γ which are contained in A k and whose endpoints lie on ∂A k are a.s. determined by Ψ | A k . For all k ∈ N 0 , we let Γ out k (resp. Γ in k ) denote the collection of segments of loops in Γ which make an excursion into A k from the outer (resp. inner) boundary of A k . We also let Γ I k denote the collection of segments of loops in Γ which cross A k .

We are now ready to define the main event that we are going to consider.

Definition A.6. Fix δ ∈ (0 , 1) small and N ∈ N 0 large (both deterministic). For all k ∈ N 0 , we let E k = E k (Ψ) denote the event that the following hold.

- (i) The number of crossings in Γ I k is at most N .
- (ii) There exists c ∈ 2 λ Z such that the boundary conditions of Ψ on the left and right sides of any segment ℓ in Γ I k lie in [ c - N,c + N ].

̸

- (iv) For all ℓ ∈ Γ I k , we have that the harmonic measure as seen from 0 of the part of the left (resp. right) side of ℓ contained in ℓ \ ( ⋃ ℓ ′ ∈ Γ out k ℓ ′ ) in the connected component of D \ ( ⋃ γ ∈ Γ out k ∪ Γ I k γ ) containing 0 is at least δ .
- (iii) For all ℓ, ℓ ′ ∈ Γ I k such that ℓ = ℓ ′ , we have that dist( ℓ, ℓ ′ ) ≥ δ 2 - 3 k .

Note that Lemma A.5 implies that E k is a.s. determined by Ψ | A k . Hence, by absolute continuity, we can define the events E k ( ˜ Ψ) in the same way for any field ˜ Ψ on D whose boundary conditions are piecewise constant and change only countably many times, with the segments of loops replaced by the corresponding level lines of ˜ Ψ. Let us emphasize that condition (ii) is formulated relative to an unspecified c ∈ 2 λ Z , so that E k ( ˜ Ψ) = E k ( ˜ Ψ - 2 λm ) for every m ∈ Z . This invariance is essential: the heights of the crossings of A k perform a random walk in k , so that an absolute bound on them would fail for all but O ( √ k ) of the first k scales.

Condition (i) will allow us to reduce the claim of Proposition A.1 to proving estimates on the probability of four-arm events for at most N level lines of Ψ. Moreover, condition (ii) will be useful since it will allow us to compare the law of a field with complicated boundary conditions that can change infinitely many times with the law of a field whose boundary conditions are simpler and whose level lines with appropriate heights have the same joint law as the level lines appearing in the statement of Proposition A.4. As for conditions (iii) and (iv), they will ensure that the level lines discovered by the exploration are sufficiently far away from each other so that Proposition A.4 can be applied to those level lines.

If we choose the constants δ, N appearing in the definition of E k (Ψ) appropriately, we have that the event E k (Ψ) occurs for many values of k with very high probability. This is the content of the following lemma.

Lemma A.7. Fix a ∈ (0 , 1) . Then, we can choose δ, ε 0 ∈ (0 , 1) sufficiently small and N ∈ N 0 sufficiently large (depending only on a ) such that the following holds for all ε ∈ (0 , ε 0 ) . Off an event with probability at most ε 3 , we have that there exists k ∈ [1 , a log(1 /ε )] Z such that E k (Ψ) occurs.

Proof. First, we note that by the definition of the exploration, we have that the crossings in Γ I 0 are a.s. pairwise disjoint and that a.s.,

Moreover, the boundary conditions of Ψ on the left and right sides of any segment in Γ I 0 lie in some compact interval since the exploration of the segments terminates after finitely many steps a.s. Thus, since the number of crossings in Γ I 0 is a.s. finite (by the local finiteness property of nested CLE 4 ), we obtain that for all p ∈ (0 , 1), we can choose δ ∈ (0 , 1) sufficiently small and N ∈ N sufficiently large such that P [ E 0 (Ψ)] ≥ p . We now transfer this to all scales. Let us apply Lemma 5.5 with F = B 1 / 2 (0) and z = 0, with K def = ⌊ 3 a log(1 /ε ) ⌋ , and with its two parameters (which we denote here by α and β 0 , so as not to clash with the constants a and b of this appendix) chosen so that β 0 ∈ (5 / 6 , 1) and α is large depending on a . It follows that off an event of probability at most ε 3 there are at most (1 - β 0 ) K values of j ∈ [1 , K ] Z for which B 2 - j (0) fails to be M -good. Since the number of j ∈ [1 , K ] which are divisible by 3 is at least K/ 3 - 2 and 1 - β 0 &lt; 1 / 6, we deduce that there are at least c 1 a log(1 /ε ) values of k ∈ [1 , a log(1 /ε )] Z for which B 2 - 3 k (0) is M -good, where c 1 &gt; 0 is universal. For each such k we have A k ⊆ B 15 · 2 - 3 k / 16 (0), and E k (Ψ) = E k (Ψ - ⌊ Ψ 2 - 3 k (0) / (2 λ ) ⌋ 2 λ ) by the invariance noted after the definition of E k ; hence Lemma 5.6 together with Hölder's inequality and the scale invariance of the law of the zero-boundary GFF gives P [ E k (Ψ) | F 0 , 3 k ] ≥ p ′ a.s. on E M 0 , 3 k , where p ′ ∈ (0 , 1) depends only on p and M . Since E j (Ψ) is F 0 , 3 k -measurable for j &lt; k , iterating over the good scales gives that the probability that none of the events E k (Ψ), k ∈ [1 , a log(1 /ε )] Z , occurs is at most (1 - p ′ ) c 1 a log(1 /ε ) = ε c 1 a log(1 / (1 - p ′ )) , which is at most ε 3 once p is chosen close enough to 1 in a way depending only on a . □

Let k f denote the smallest integer k ∈ [1 , a log(1 /ε )] Z for which E k (Ψ) occurs. Note that Lemma A.7 implies that k f exists with very high probability as ε → 0. Let also D 1 denote the connected component of C \ ⋃ ℓ ∈ Γ out k f ∪ Γ I k f ℓ containing 0 and note that D 1 is a simply connected domain (see Figure 10). Indeed, a.s., Lebesgue-a.e. point z of the circle ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 1 (0) lies on no loop of Γ, and the loops of Γ surrounding such a point z shrink to z . Any such loop γ of sufficiently small diameter must intersect both connected components of C \ ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 1 (0) (the interior of γ is an open set of diameter at most diam( γ ) which contains z , and hence meets both components; if γ avoided the open ball, then the ball, being connected, disjoint from γ , and meeting the interior of γ , would be contained in that interior, which has smaller diameter, while if γ avoided the complement of the closed ball, then the latter would lie in the unbounded complementary component of γ and could not meet the interior of γ ); hence the excursions of γ into B 2 - 3 k f - 1 (0) near z are excursions into A k f from its outer boundary. Therefore ⋃ ℓ ∈ Γ out k f ∪ Γ I k f ℓ contains a dense subset of the circle ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 1 (0), and hence the whole circle; since every segment in Γ out k f ∪ Γ I k f has an endpoint on that circle, this set is connected, and it separates 0 from ∞ . Moreover, it is easy to see that ∂D 1 is locally connected. Thus, there exists a conformal transformation f 1 mapping D 1 onto D such that f 1 (0) = 0 and f ′ 1 (0) &gt; 0, and f 1 extends continuously to a map from D 1 onto D . Furthermore, for the rest of the section, we will assume that Γ I = ∅ , i.e., there are loops in Γ crossing A .

k f k f

In the following lemma, we show that the boundary conditions of Ψ | D 1 lie on a fixed and deterministic compact interval. We will use this fact in Sections A.3 and A.4.

Figure 10. Illustration of the domain D 1 in blue with its boundary in red.

<!-- image -->

Lemma A.8. Suppose that we have the same setup described above, and work on the event that k f exists. Then, writing c ∈ 2 λ Z for the constant furnished by condition (ii) of E k f (Ψ) , the boundary conditions of the field Ψ | D 1 lie in [ c - N - 4 λ, c + N + 4 λ ] ; in particular, upon replacing N by ⌈ N +4 λ ⌉ , we may and do assume that they lie in [ c - N,c + N ] .

Proof. Step 1. Reduction to a single excursion. Fix ℓ ∈ Γ out k f such that ℓ ⊆ ∂D 1 and let γ ∈ Γ be such that ℓ ⊆ γ . We will show that the boundary conditions of Ψ on the left and right sides of ℓ lie in [ c - N - 4 λ, c + N +4 λ ]. This will complete the proof since we already know by (ii) that the boundary conditions of Ψ on ∂D 1 ∩ ( ⋃ ℓ ′ ∈ Γ I k f ℓ ′ ) lie in [ c - N,c + N ]. All of the intervals appearing below are to be read relative to c in the same way; the argument is purely relative, since it only ever compares the boundary conditions on two loops whose nesting generations differ by one. We will treat the following cases separately, according to the position of γ relative to ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0).

Step 2. The case where γ does not meet ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0) and does not surround it. If γ is an outermost loop in Γ, then by the construction of the coupling (Ψ , Γ) we have that the boundary conditions of Ψ on γ are contained in {- 2 λ, 0 , 2 λ } . In this case γ is an outermost loop which meets ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 1 (0); the claim therefore holds after increasing N by a universal constant.

Suppose now that γ is not an outermost loop in Γ and let γ ′ be a loop in Γ that surrounds γ such that the nesting generation of γ ′ is given by n - 1, where n denotes the nesting generation of γ . If γ ′ crosses A k f , then the boundary conditions of Ψ on γ ′ lie in [ c - N,c + N ], and so the construction of the coupling (Ψ , Γ) implies that the boundary conditions of Ψ on γ lie in [ c - N - 2 λ, c + N +2 λ ]. If γ ′ does not cross A k f , then we must have that ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0) lies in the bounded connected component of C \ γ ′ , since otherwise we would have that γ ′ disconnects γ from ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0), and that contradicts the definition of Γ out k f . Hence, the loop γ ′ surrounds B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0). This implies that γ ′ surrounds all the loops in Γ which cross A k f .

̸

Let ˜ γ be a loop in Γ which crosses A k f and which is outermost among those loops in the sense that there is no other loop in Γ which crosses A k f and surrounds ˜ γ . We claim that the nesting generation of ˜ γ is given by n , i.e., ˜ γ and γ have the same generation. Indeed, suppose that ˜ γ and γ do not have the same generation. Then, there exists a loop ˆ γ ∈ Γ such that ˆ γ disconnects ˜ γ from γ . Note that ˆ γ ∩ ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 1 (0) = ∅ since ˆ γ surrounds ˜ γ and note that ˆ γ does not cross A k f due to the choice of ˜ γ . Thus, we must have that ˆ γ disconnects γ from ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0) but that contradicts the definition of Γ out k f together with the fact that ℓ ⊆ ∂D 1 . It follows that ˜ γ and γ have the same nesting generation. In particular, the construction of the coupling (Ψ , Γ) implies that the boundary conditions of Ψ on γ ′ lie in [ c - N - 2 λ, c + N +2 λ ], which also implies that the boundary conditions of Ψ on γ lie in [ c - N - 4 λ, c + N +4 λ ], as required.

Step 3. The case where γ does not meet ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0) and surrounds it. In that case, the loop γ surrounds B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0). Moreover, all of the loops in Γ which cross A k f are surrounded by γ ; we let ˜ γ be a loop in Γ which crosses A k f and which is outermost in the same sense as in Step 2. Then, if n is the nesting generation of γ , we claim that the nesting generation of ˜ γ is given by n + 1. Indeed, suppose that this is not the case. Then, there exists a loop ˆ γ ∈ Γ such that ˆ γ surrounds ˜ γ and ˆ γ is surrounded by γ . Since we have that ˆ γ ∩ ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0) = ∅ (by the outermost nature of ˜ γ ), we obtain that ˆ γ surrounds B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0), and hence ˆ γ disconnects ℓ from ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0). But this contradicts the fact that ℓ ⊆ ∂D 1 . It follows that the nesting generation of ˜ γ is given by n +1 and so the boundary conditions of Ψ on γ lie in [ c - N - 2 λ, c + N +2 λ ].

Step 4. The case where γ meets ∂B 2 - 3 k f - 2 (0) . In that case, we have that γ crosses A k f and thus (ii) implies that the boundary conditions of Ψ on γ lie in [ c - N,c + N ]. Therefore, the proof of the lemma is complete by combining with the previous cases. □

## A.3. Bounding the probability that level lines intersect ∂B ε (0) without intersecting each other.

A.3.1. Outline and setup. For the rest of the section, we fix a ∈ (0 , 1 / 100) small and assume that δ ∈ (0 , 1) , N ∈ N are chosen so that the statement of Lemma A.7 holds. We also fix points x 1 , x 2 , x 3 , and x 4 on ∂D 1 which are endpoints of crossings contained in Γ I k f , labeled in counterclockwise order on ∂D 1 . Let η 1 , η 2 , η 3 , η 4 be the crossings in Γ I k f which terminate at x 1 , x 2 , x 3 , x 4 respectively. We also assume that there exist k ∈ Z and σ 1 , σ 3 ∈ {- 1 , +1 } such that the following holds for j ∈ { 1 , 3 } . The boundary conditions of Ψ on the left (resp. right) side of η j are given by 2 λk (resp. 2 λ ( k + σ j )), and on the left (resp. right) side of η j +1 are given by 2 λ ( k + σ j ) (resp. 2 λk ); equivalently, η j (resp. η j +1 ) corresponds to a level line of σ j Ψ (resp. - σ j Ψ) with height σ j (2 k + σ j ) λ (resp. - σ j (2 k + σ j ) λ ).

The above assumption corresponds, after subtracting the constant 2 λk from Ψ, to the setup of Proposition A.4 with the same value of ( σ 1 , σ 3 ): the height σ j (2 k + σ j ) λ of η j becomes σ j (2 k + σ j ) λ - 2 kσ j λ = λ for the field σ j (Ψ - 2 λk ), as in the proposition. The reason for restricting attention to crossings as above is the following. Suppose that ℓ, ℓ ′ ∈ Γ are distinct loops so that both of them cross C ε and so that either ℓ, ℓ ′ have the same generation, or one of the loops surrounds the other and their nesting generations differ by 1. Then there are four crossings in Γ I k f which merge with ℓ or with ℓ ′ , and, since ℓ and ℓ ′ are disjoint, the two which merge with the same loop have adjacent endpoints in the counterclockwise order on ∂D 1 . Labeling them η 1 , η 2 , η 3 , η 4 in counterclockwise order in such a way that η 1 , η 2 merge with ℓ ′ and η 3 , η 4 merge with ℓ , the construction of the coupling (Ψ , Γ) implies that the above assumption holds for some k ∈ Z and σ 1 , σ 3 ∈ {- 1 , +1 } . Let us stress that it is the pairing of the crossings which forces the labeling. Indeed, the two arcs of ∂D 1 between η 2 and η 3 and between η 4 and η 1 are the ones lying outside both ℓ and ℓ ′ (in the case that ℓ surrounds ℓ ′ , outside ℓ ′ but inside ℓ ), so that the boundary conditions of Ψ on both of them are equal to 2 λk , exactly as the above assumption requires. Under a cyclic shift of the labels by one, the two crossings labeled η 1 and η 2 would be arms of different loops, and the resulting boundary conditions need not satisfy the above assumption. Similarly, if the loop ℓ ∈ Γ makes four crossings of C ε and η 1 , η 2 , η 3 , η 4 are the crossings in Γ I k f which merge with ℓ , labeled in counterclockwise order, we have that the above assumption holds. We will see in Section A.4 why it suffices to consider crossings of C ε made by loops ℓ, ℓ ′ in Γ as above.

Next, let us fix the hierarchy of the constants, which we shall use throughout. We first fix a as above and a ratio s ∈ (0 , 1); next δ ∈ (0 , 1) small and N ∈ N large, depending only on a and s ; next b ∈ (0 , a/ 100) depending only on a ; next ρ ∈ (0 , a ) small, depending only on a, b, δ, N ; and finally ζ def = sρ/ 2 ∈ (0 , ρ/ 2). In the proofs of Propositions A.1 and A.2 we shall let s → 0 and then a → 0. For all ε ∈ (0 , 1), we let n ε denote the largest positive integer for which it holds that

Lemma A.11. Suppose that we have the same setup described above. Then, for all ρ and ζ as above and all n ∈ N , the following holds. Conditional on the event that η j,m intersects ∂B ( ε ρ/ 2+6 a log(2) / 64 2 )( ε ρ/ 2+ ζ / 16 2 ) m - 1 (0) for the first time before intersecting η k,m for all 1 ≤ m ≤ n and all j, k ∈ { 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } such that j = k , the conditional probability that, for every j ∈ { 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } , the path η j,n +1 intersects ∂B ( ε ρ/ 2+6 a log(2) / 64 2 )( ε ρ/ 2+ ζ / 16 2 ) n (0) before intersecting any of the other three paths η k,n +1 , is at most ε ρ (1 - a ) for all ε ∈ (0 , ε 0 ) , where ε 0 ∈ (0 , 1) depends only on a, δ, N, ρ , and ζ .

A.3.2. Proving Lemma A.10. Next, we prove Lemma A.10. First, we set Ψ 1 = Ψ ◦ f - 1 1 , and for all j ∈ { 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } , we set ˜ x j = f 1 ( x j ) and let γ j be the corresponding level line of either Ψ 1 or - Ψ 1 starting from ˜ x j and having the same height as η j .

We will use Proposition A.4 in order to prove Lemma A.10. We start with the following lemma which proves that with very high probability, we have that the tips of the level lines η 1 , η 2 , η 3 , η 4 become sufficiently separated if we draw the curves a bit further.

Lemma A.12. Let δ, N be the constants in the statement of Lemma A.7. Then, by possibly taking δ ∈ (0 , 1) to be smaller and N to be larger (in a way that depends only on a ), we have that the following holds off an event whose probability is O ( ε 3 ) as ε → 0 (at a rate depending only on a, δ , and N ). There exists n ∈ [ a log(1 /ε ) / 2 , a log(1 /ε )] Z such that the event E n (Ψ 1 ) occurs.

Proof. First, we note that Lemma A.8 together with [MS16a, Proposition 3.4] implies that for any compact set K ⊆ D , the conditional law of (Ψ 1 - c ) | K given D 1 is absolutely continuous with respect to the corresponding restriction to K of a zero-boundary GFF on D , and that the p -th moments of the corresponding Radon-Nikodym derivatives are bounded from below and above by constants that depend only on p, K , and N . Since the events E n (Ψ 1 ) are invariant under the addition of an element of 2 λ Z to the field, and since c ∈ 2 λ Z , this comparison may be applied directly to them. Therefore, taking K = B 1 / 2 (0) and combining this with the argument used to prove Lemma A.7, we complete the proof of the lemma. □

For the rest of the section, we will assume that there exists n ∈ [ a log(1 /ε ) / 2 , a log(1 /ε )] Z such that the event E n (Ψ 1 ) occurs, and we let ˜ k f be the smallest such integer n . One difficulty in applying Proposition A.4 is that the boundary conditions of the field ˜ Ψ 1 are not consistent with the boundary conditions of the field appearing in the statement of Proposition A.4. ## A.4. Conclusion of the proof.

A.4.1. Proof of Proposition A.1 assuming Proposition A.4. Now, we are ready to prove Proposition A.1. Suppose that there are at least four crossings of C ε made by loops in Γ; then either there are two distinct loops ℓ, ℓ ′ ∈ Γ which both cross C ε , or a single loop ℓ makes at least four crossings of C ε . As we mentioned in Section A.3.1, in the former case it suffices to consider the case that either int( ℓ ) ∩ int( ℓ ′ ) = ∅ and ℓ, ℓ ′ have the same nesting generation, or one of the loops surrounds the other and their nesting generations differ by one. Indeed, suppose that int( ℓ ) ∩ int( ℓ ′ ) = ∅ and that the two loops have different nesting generations. Without loss of generality, we can assume that the nesting generation of ℓ is larger than that of ℓ ′ . Then, there exists a loop ˜ ℓ ∈ Γ such that ˜ ℓ surrounds ℓ , and the loops ℓ ′ and ˜ ℓ have the same level of nesting. Thus, since both ℓ, ℓ ′ cross C ε , the same holds for the loops ℓ ′ , ˜ ℓ . (Indeed, int( ˜ ℓ ) contains the connected set ℓ ∪ int( ℓ ), which joins the two boundary circles of C ε , so that either ˜ ℓ crosses C ε or ˜ ℓ surrounds B 1 / 2 (0); the latter is impossible, since it would force ℓ ′ ⊆ int( ˜ ℓ ) and hence ˜ ℓ and ℓ ′ would not have the same nesting generation.) Furthermore, if ℓ, ℓ ′ are such that int( ℓ ) ⊆ int( ℓ ′ ) and the levels of nesting of ℓ and ℓ ′ differ by more than 1, then there exists a loop ˜ ℓ ∈ Γ such that int( ℓ ) ⊆ int( ˜ ℓ ) ⊆ int( ℓ ′ ) and the levels of nesting of ˜ ℓ and ℓ ′ differ by 1. Hence, if both ℓ and ℓ ′ cross C ε , then the same holds for ˜ ℓ and ℓ ′ .

We claim that it suffices to show that, on the event that there are at least four crossings of C ε made by loops in Γ, the event F ε in the statement of Lemma A.9 occurs. Indeed, note that by (A.1), for all sufficiently small ε we have that

We may therefore apply Lemma A.9 together with the fact that the total number of crossings in Γ I k f is at most N (so that there are at most 4 ( N 4 ) counterclockwise labelings of a quadruple η 1 , η 2 , η 3 , η 4 , and a union bound costs only a constant factor) to complete the proof. Step 2. The four crossings and their boundary conditions. Suppose that both ℓ and ℓ ′ cross C ε . There exists k ∈ Z such that if the nesting generations of ℓ and ℓ ′ are the same, then the boundary conditions of Ψ on the boundaries of the unbounded connected components of C \ int( ℓ ) and C \ int( ℓ ′ ) are both given by 2 λk (hence the boundary conditions of Ψ | int( ℓ ) and Ψ | int( ℓ ′ ) lie in { 2 λ ( k - 1) , 2 λ ( k + 1) } ), and if one of the loops surrounds the other (we can assume that ℓ surrounds ℓ ′ ), it holds that the boundary conditions of Ψ | int( ℓ ) and those of Ψ on the boundary of the unbounded connected component of C \ int( ℓ ′ ) are both given by 2 λk . Moreover, in the latter case, we have that both the boundary conditions of Ψ on the boundary of the unbounded connected component of C \ int( ℓ ) and the boundary conditions of Ψ | int( ℓ ′ ) lie in { 2 λ ( k - 1) , 2 λ ( k +1) } .

Step 3. Conclusion. Note that the construction of the coupling (Ψ , Γ) combined with the level line interaction rules implies that each crossing of C ε made by ℓ or by ℓ ′ has to merge with one of the crossings in Γ I k f . Therefore, we obtain that there exist crossings η 1 , η 2 , η 3 , η 4 of Γ I k f as above such that η 1 and η 2 (resp. η 3 and η 4 ) are traced by ℓ ′ (resp. ℓ ) while making one crossing from ∂B 1 / 2 (0) to ∂B ε (0) and another crossing from ∂B ε (0) to ∂B 1 / 2 (0). Also, in the case that ℓ makes four crossings of C ε , then there exist k ∈ Z and crossings η 1 , η 2 , η 3 , η 4 as above such that all of η 1 , η 2 , η 3 , η 4 are traced by ℓ . Therefore, in either case, the loops must agree with the corresponding level line crossings up until the latter intersect ∂B ε (0) for the first time. Since the choice of n ε implies that

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

we obtain that the event F ε occurs; here we use that η 1 and η 2 (resp. η 3 and η 4 ) are the two strands of the same loop ℓ ′ (resp. ℓ ), so that they do not meet before that loop has completed its crossings of C ε , while η 1 , η 2 are disjoint from η 3 , η 4 since ℓ and ℓ ′ are distinct loops. This completes the proof of the proposition. □

Proof of Proposition A.2. Step 1. Construction of the shields. First, we note that combining Lemmas A.9 and A.19, we obtain that off an event with probability at most ε 3 + ε ρn ε (1 - a )+ b 0 as ε → 0 (at a rate that depends only on a, δ, N, ρ , and θ ), we have that the following holds. Suppose that the event F ε in the statement of Lemma A.9 occurs. Then, for all j ∈ { 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } , there exists n j ∈ { 0 , 1 , . . . , ⌊ log(1 /ε ) ⌋ - 1 } such that the curve ϕ - 1 j ( P j,n j ) intersects η j +1 before intersecting ∂U j \ ( η j ∪ η j +1 ) for the first time. Suppose that we are working on the above event.

Step 2. Conclusion. Suppose that there are at least four crossings of C ε made by loops in Γ. Then, as explained in the proof of Proposition A.1, the event F ε occurs. In particular, there exist level lines η 1 , η 2 , η 3 , η 4 as in the definition of F ε , such that at least four crossings of C ε made by loops in Γ merge with η 1 , η 2 , η 3 , and η 4 respectively. We claim that there are no other crossings of C ε made by loops in Γ. Indeed, suppose that there exists ℓ ∈ Γ such that ℓ ∩ ( ∪ 4 i =1 η i ) = ∅ and ℓ crosses C ε . Then, there exists j ∈ { 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } such that ℓ makes a crossing in U j between the two connected components of ∂U j \ ( η j ∪ η j +1 ). But then, ℓ has to cross P j,n j and this contradicts the level line interaction rules since level lines do not cross each other. It follows that there are at most four crossings of C ε made by loops in Γ. Therefore, we conclude the proof of the proposition as in the proof of Proposition A.1 by first taking s → 0 and then a → 0, and noting that b 0 is independent of a, ζ, ρ , and θ ; more precisely, we choose a and s small enough that ρn ε (1 - a ) ≥ 2 - b 0 / 2, and then the probability bound ε 3 + ε ρn ε (1 - a )+ b 0 is at most 2 ε 2+ b 0 / 2 since b 0 &lt; 1 &lt; 2, which gives the statement of the proposition upon replacing b 0 by b 0 / 4. □

A.4.3. Proof of Proposition A.4. Before we give the proof of Proposition A.4, we give some background on bi-chordal SLE 4 processes.

Let D be a simply connected domain and fix prime ends x 1 , x 2 , x 3 , x 4 ∈ ∂D ordered in the counterclockwise way. A bi-chordal SLE 4 on D with link pattern {{ x 1 , x 2 } , { x 3 , x 4 }} is a probability measure on pairs of simple curves ( η 12 , η 34 ) such that η 12 (resp. η 34 ) connects x 1 to x 2 (resp. x 3 to x 4 ) in D and the curves η 12 , η 34 do not intersect each other, and such that the following is true: The law of η 12 (resp. η 34 ) given η 34 (resp. η 12 ) is that of a chordal SLE 4 from x 1 to x 2 (resp. from x 3 to x 4 ) on the connected component of D \ η 34 (resp. D \ η 12 ) whose boundary contains x 1 and x 2 (resp. x 3 and x 4 ).

The existence of bi-chordal SLE 4 was proved in [KL07] using the Brownian loop soup measure. The uniqueness of bi-chordal SLE 4 follows from the exact same argument used to prove [MS16b, Theorem 4.1]. In particular, the authors in [MS16b] proved the uniqueness of bi-chordal SLE κ for all κ ∈ (0 , 4) (for a fixed domain and a link pattern), using the theory of flow lines introduced in [MS16a]. However, the exact same argument can be applied in the case of bi-chordal SLE 4 where the results in [MS16a] are replaced by the corresponding results in the context of level lines proved in [WW17]. See also [BPW21, Theorem 1.2] for another proof for all κ ∈ (0 , 4].

Proposition A.20. Suppose that we have the same setup as in Proposition A.4. Let E denote the event that P 1 and P 2 merge and that P 3 and P 4 merge, in each case before either of the two curves in question reaches its continuation threshold. Then, the conditional law given E of the pair ( P 1 , P 3 ) (equivalently, of the pair ( P 2 , P 4 ) , since on E these are the time-reversals of P 1 and P 3 , respectively) is that of a bi-chordal SLE 4 with link pattern {{ z 1 , z 2 } , { z 3 , z 4 }} .

Proof. Let E 1 (resp. E 2 ) denote the event that the curves P 1 , P 2 (resp. P 3 , P 4 ) merge before either of them reaches its continuation threshold. Let also E = E 1 ∩ E 2 . Note that [WW17, Theorem 1.1.6] implies that on the event E 1 (resp. E 2 ), the curve P 2 (resp. P 4 ) is equal to the time-reversal of P 1 (resp. P 3 ) a.s. Moreover, if E 1 occurs, the level line interaction rules imply that both P 3 and P 4 stay to the left of P 1 . In particular, since both of the force points of P 3 and P 4 with weight at most - 2 lie on the boundary of the connected component of D \ P 1 lying to the right of P 1 , we obtain that none of the curves P 3 , P 4 hit their continuation threshold. Hence E 1 ⊆ E 2 , and by symmetry E 2 ⊆ E 1 ; it follows that E = E 1 = E 2 . Also, [MW17, Lemmas 2.3, 2.5] implies that P [ E ] &gt; 0.

Next, we note that the boundary conditions of h combined with the choice of the paths P 1 , P 2 , P 3 and P 4 , together imply that the conditional law of P 3 given ( P 1 , P 2 ) and on the event E 1 , is that of a chordal SLE 4 from z 3 to z 4 on the connected component of D \ P 1 lying to the left of P 1 . Similarly, we have that the conditional law of P 1 given ( P 3 , P 4 ) and on the event E 2 , is that of a chordal SLE 4 from z 1 to z 2 on the connected component of D \ P 3 lying on the left of P 3 . Note that since E = E 1 = E 2 , we have that E is a.s. determined by P 1 (resp. P 3 ). It thus follows from combining with the uniqueness of the law of bi-chordal SLE 4 that the conditional law of ( P 1 , P 3 ) (resp. ( P 2 , P 4 )) given E is that of a bi-chordal SLE 4 on D with link pattern {{ z 1 , z 2 } , { z 3 , z 4 }} . □

Proof of Proposition A.4. Let E be the event in the statement of Proposition A.20. The event in the statement of the proposition is contained in E , and on it the two curves P 1 ∪ P 2 and P 3 ∪ P 4 both intersect B ε (0) without intersecting each other. Therefore, by Proposition A.20 and Lemma 2.7, the probability of that event is at most P [ E ] times the probability that both curves of a bi-chordal SLE 4 with link pattern {{ z 1 , z 2 } , { z 3 , z 4 }} intersect B 2 ε (0), which is at most Cε 2 for a universal constant C . □
